>Morrison Foerster, a top global law firm, announced the addition of two new partners in the firm's Intellectual Property Litigation Group and Life Sciences + Healthcare divisions. Ching Lee Fukuda and Tom Broughan provide nearly five decades of combined experience in patent and trade secret law regarding medical devices, medtech, life sciences, and technology.

Fukuda previously served as Sidley Austin’s co-leader of global IP litigation and a member of their Global Life Sciences leadership team. She was a partner there for over a decade, winning trial victories before district courts, the Federal Circuit, the International Trade Commission and the Patent Trial and Appeal Board.  Fukuda’s work has been recognized by many legal publications, and she was named Patent Lawyer of the Year by Women in Business Law Americas in 2024.

Broughan also joins Morrison Foerster from Sidley Austin, bringing similar experience in IP litigation in district court. In Patent Trial and Appeal Board proceedings, he regularly serves as a lead counsel and has been recognized in Best Lawyers for his work there. The two of them have worked together extensively, according to Morrison Foerster’s press release.

Brian Nash, the chair of Morrison Foerster’s global Intellectual Property Group, notes that Fukuda and Broughan will continue to work together to “enhance and expand our IP litigation footprint.”

>According to a recent LinkedIn post, Maynd Jolly has been named the CFO of Health Care Delivery for CVS Health. Previously, he worked as CFO and SVP of Growth and Client Partnerships at enGen, and Global CFO of Digital Oncology and Head of Finance at Enterprise Services.

> The American Medical Association has named Sandra Adamson Fryhofer, M.D., as its next president-elect, a decision that comes as the current holder of that title, Willie Underwood III, M.D., takes the place of outgoing president Bobby Mukkamala, M.D.

Fryhofer is a board-certified internal medicine physician with a full-time practice in Atlanta and an adjust associate professor of medicine role at Emory University School of Medicine. She's been a member of the group's House of Delegates since 1999, elected to its Board of Trustees in 2018 and served as the board's chair from 2022 to 2023. She also previously had leading roles at the American College of Physicians as its president and a member of its Board of Regents.

Fryhofer has been a prominent voice in public health. Alongside serving as the AMA's liaison to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention’s Advisory Committee on Immunization Practices (CDC ACIP), she has been a regular medical correspondent and contributor for CNN and Medscape.

“Medicine is at its best when we put patients first and remain guided by science and evidence," Fryhofer said in the organization's announcement. "We have a tremendous opportunity to improve public health and ensure every patient has access to high-quality care. I am honored to serve as president-elect as we continue that work together.”

The AMA's members elect presidents for one-year terms that turnover every June during its Annual Meeting of the House of Delegates. Underwood, a urologic surgeon with expertise in healthcare disparities and policy, was selected in last year's vote and will begin his term this week.

>Anant Vinjamoori, M.D., will serve as the new Chief Medical Officer for the Hims brand, an online comprehensive care platform. With more than a decade of clinical and executive experience, Vinjamoori brings expertise in preventive and longevity care that incorporates diagnostic and therapeutic technologies.

Prior to joining Hims, he founded Next Generation Medicine, which combines medical education with AI technology to assist clinicians with incorporating longevity medicine. He is also the author of several peer-reviewed publications, and Vinjamoori received his MD from Harvard Medical School and his MBA from Harvard Business School.

>The Melanoma Research Alliance (MRA), a nonprofit funder of melanoma research, announced that beginning on July 1, 2026, Stephanie Kauffmann will serve as the organization's Chief Executive Officer. Joining MRA in 2022 as Chief Operating Officer and President, she oversaw growth in research investment and philanthropic and corporate partnerships.

Kauffman will replace Marc Hulbert, Ph.D., who is leaving MRA to become CEO of the National Kidney Foundation. The Board recognized Dr. Hulbert’s contribution to strengthening the position of MRA as a melanoma research funding leader, and notes that he will continue to serve as a member of MRA's Scientific Advisory Panel.

According to Debra Black, Co-Founder and Chair of the Melanoma Research Alliance, in Kauffman’s previous role she “has earned the trust and respect of our Board, staff, researchers, donors, and patient community through her leadership, vision, and unwavering commitment to our mission.” Under Kauffman’s leadership, MRA plans to continue expanding their investments in groundbreaking rare melanoma research and patient centered innovation to improve conditions for patients worldwide.

>Ardent Health President and CEO Marty Bonick "has stepped down to pursue other opportunities," the for-profit health system announced this week. Replacing him immediately is Dave Caspers, who had been serving as chief operating officer.

Bonick helmed the company through a string of challenges, including the COVID-19 pandemic and its 2024 initial public offering. Board Chairman Mark Sotir noted that work in the announcement and said the former head executive had positioned Ardent "for sustained long-term growth and help[ed] build a strong foundation as the Company continues to navigate industry headwinds with a focus on disciplined execution. We wish him all the best in his next chapter."

Caspers, meanwhile, joined Ardent in March 2025 but has spent decades in operational roles. These include senior healthcare leadership positions at Walmart Health, Banner Health and Target. He's spent the past year in the weeds of Ardent's IMPACT (Improve Margins, Performance, Agility and Care Transformation) Program.

"Over the past year, I have had the privilege of working closely with Marty, whose leadership and guidance have been instrumental in laying the solid footing on which Ardent Health now stands," said Caspers, who has also joined Ardent's board. "Today, Ardent Health and our best-in-class team are well-positioned to accelerate our next phase of growth, and I am eager to build on this momentum with a continued focus on delivering high-quality care to the communities we serve.”

>Drew Altman, M.D., founding president and CEO of the influential heath policy research, polling and news nonprofit KFF, will retire from his roles at the end of this year.

He formed the organization in 1990 out of the Henry J. Kaiser Family Foundation, and has since expanded its scope to include the country's largest newsroom focused on health policy, KFF Health News.

“With two small grandkids in Sacramento and Atlanta and another on the way, after what will be almost 37 years of very hands-on leadership since I founded KFF, and with KFF at the apex of its effectiveness, and the opportunity for a perfect transition, the clock in my head is telling me it’s time,” Altman said in this week's announcement.

Following the recommendation of a search committee, KFF has unanimously appointed two in-house leaders to take on Altman's titles: Larry Levitt, currently executive vice president for health policy, as CEO; and Mollyann Brodie, Ph.D., currently executive vice president and chief operating officer, as president.

The pair have a combined six decades of experience at KFF already under their belt, and will take on board positions with their new roles. They will work with Altman to ensure a smooth transition through the end of the year, officially stepping into his shoes in January 2027

>Visory Health, a prescription savings program, has promoted two executives: Alexandra Robertson to chief growth officer, and Brittney Tierce to chief technology officer.

Robertson started as vice president of growth, driving the company's expansion and presence in more than 38,000 pharmacies nationwide. She will now oversee growth strategy, partnership development and program innovation.

"Visory is positioned to redefine what prescription access looks like for underserved populations, making affordability and access the norm, not the exception," Robertson said in a press release shared in advance with Fierce Healthcare.

Meanwhile, Tierce created the tech underlying Visory. She will now drive the tech strategy company-wide, including security, tech and AI innovation.

"Technology should be a bridge, not a barrier," Tierce said in the announcement. "My focus is raising that bar continuously by creating smarter systems, faster delivery and infrastructure that scales access to the communities we serve."

> Healthcare AI company Abridge brought on board a new chief technology officer, San Oo, as it expands its technology into more health systems. Oo joins Abridge after previously holding leadership roles at Slack and Notion, two popular workplace and productivity software tools.

At Notion, Oo set engineering goals and directions, with a strong focus on reliability, developer velocity and agentic engineering, according to an Abridge press release. At Slack, he helped build the engineering organization during the "hypergrowth years" that defined modern workplace collaboration. Earlier, he served as CTO at ShopBack, a unicorn in the Asia-Pacific region. He was also a founding member of two companies that were later acquired: Mumbo Inc., acquired by LinkedIn in 2012, and Astro Technology, acquired by Slack in 2018.

"The platforms San helped build altered the texture of work with thoughtful technology and exceptional craft that facilitate human connection,” Shiv Rao, M.D., CEO and founder of Abridge, said in a statement. “Healthcare is the next industry that needs that kind of care and platform-level rethink, and San is the rare engineering leader who's done it multiple times."

Abridge is rapidly scaling its AI scribe and clinical decision support technology. It is live in more than 270 of the largest health systems in the U.S., including Duke Health, Johns Hopkins Medicine, Sutter Health and Yale New Haven Health. The platform will support more than 100 million patient conversations in 2026.

"I've spent my career building products and platforms that people love to use that are secure and reliable. Now I want to do the same for healthcare, where the stakes are even higher,” Oo said in a statement. “Abridge already sits at the center of the most important moment in healthcare, the conversation between a clinician and a patient. The opportunity now is to extend that intelligence across the entire system."

> Alignment Healthcare has announced multiple leadership changes that it says will power up the company for long-term growth.

In an announcement, the insurtech said that CEO John Kao has also been named as chairman of the board, allowing him to work more closely with board members. Alongside this shift, previous board chair Joseph Konowiecki has been named vice chairman and as Alignment's executive vice president of corporate affairs.

In the role, Konowiecki will provide critical enterprise-wide leadership as the team looks to build scale, Alignment said in the announcement. Konowiecki is a veteran healthcare executive.

Mark Kent will also join Alignment as president of its management services organization, or MSO. Kent brings experience in value-based care and primary care to the position, having previously held leadership roles in Humana's primary care division, Alignment said.

Alignment Healthcare has also tapped Shane Hochradel as its new chief operating officer. Hochradel has held leadership positions at Elevance Health, Highmark and UnitedHealth Group, per the announcement. All three executives will report to Kao.

“Together, these leadership additions strengthen Alignment’s operating model and position the company well for sustained growth and execution at scale,” Kao said in the announcement.

> Blue Shield of California has named Hayley Park as its new senior vice president and chief pharmacy officer.

In the role, Park will lead the charge on Blue Shield's Pharmacy Care Reimagined initiative, which eschews traditional pharmacy benefit management models for an "unbundled" approach that leans on multiple solutions to offer different elements of the benefit.

Blue Shield said Park brings more than 25 years of experience to the position, and that she served most recently at Kaiser Permanente Northern California as president of pharmacy operations and services.

“What drew me to Blue Shield is that it’s a mission-driven nonprofit health plan that’s committed to improving member health and their lives—not just lowering costs for medications,” Park said. “As pharmacy care shifts to support individuals in a more connected and personalized way, I am excited to join an organization that is transforming healthcare to make it more accessible for everyone.”

> WellSpan Health President and CEO Roxanna Gapstur, Ph.D, is calling it a career after more than eight years at the nonprofit system's helm.

WellSpan's board announced the plans April 6, sharing that Gapstur will continue in the role until the transition to a replacement is complete. No specific or expected timeline was given, other than sharing that a national search for that new executive has begun.

The system, which employs 23,000 people, has grown under her tenure from eight hospitals to 12, and from $2.5 billion of revenue to $4.7 billion. Its footprint has also expanded from five counties to 12 across Pennsylvania and northern Maryland, now reaching more than 1 million patients.

The announcement credits Gapstur with bringing "clear strategic direction" that focused on quality, safety, patient experience and employee engagement. That approach brough national recognitions and designations to its facilities, plus new developments like a genomic program bringing precision medicine into patient care.

> Centene Corporation has created two new positions to lead key segments of its insurance business.

The company said in an announcement that it has named Daniel Finke as group president for markets and commercial and Michael Carson as its group president for Medicare and specialty. Both executives will report to CEO Sarah London, per Centene.

Finke is an experienced insurance executive and will be tasked with overseeing and scaling the company's Medicaid and commercial segments. He joins the insurer from Convey Health Solutions, where he was CEO, and has also previously served as president of Aetna at CVS Health.

Carson, meanwhile, will take on an expanded role in overseeing Centene's Medicare Advantage, Part D, dual-eligible and specialty populations. He joined the company as CEO of WellCare in 2024, and previously held leadership roles at Bright Healthcare and Harvard Pilgrim Health Care.

London said in the announcement that the shift recognizes Carson's significant contributions to the company and the experience that Finke brings to the table in driving operations forward.

"Their collective experience will be instrumental as we continue to strengthen performance across the portfolio and deliver sustainable, profitable growth," she said.

She'll be taking over for Peter Haytaian, who's slated to leave the role on May 4 to focus on family commitments but will remain through the end of the year as a special advisor to ease the transition, the company previously announced.

Aimée Dailey was appointed president of government business. She's already attributed with playing "a pivotal role" in expanding Elevance's government programs, and will now have responsibilities covering Medicare, Medicaid, Federal Government Solutions and Group Retiree Solutions.

Additionally, Will Feest, currently chief operating officer for Carelon, will be adding president of Carelon Insights to his title card; Darrell Oliveira was named chief financial officer for Carelon; and Jeff Plante, previously president of Carelon Insights, was dubbed chief financial officer for the Health Benefits segment.

Elevance said the new leaders will help expand and scale Carelon, which reaches over 90 million healthcare consumers. The Health Benefits segment provides coordinated solutions to more than 45 million Elevance members.

> Kurt Small was named president and CEO of CareFirst BlueCross BlueShield, effective May 4.

She's set for a promotion to president on April 5, priming her to step in as CEO in September when Berdan steps away. > Cigna Healthcare, the insurance arm of the Cigna Group, announced that it named Stanley Crittenden, M.D., as its new chief medical officer.

In the role, Crittenden will the lead the insurer's clinical operations, and will be tasked with partnering with other key leaders across the company to support the central goals of improving access and simplifying the healthcare journey for both patients and providers.

Crittenden brings years of experience in managed care, value-based care and population health to the role, Cigna said in an announcement. He most recently served as CMO at Quantum Health, and also held leadership roles at Evergreen Nephrology, Anthem and Humana.

Saul is immediately claiming the president title, and will shuffle into the top spot on Jan. 1, 2027. Haupert will stick around through 2027 in an advisory role to soften the transition, support philanthropic and capital fundraising and provide strategic guidance on a new $1+ billion medical campus the nonprofit also announced this week.

Haupert has headed Grady for the past 15 years. Saul has been around for seven, initially joining as chief financial officer.

> AI-powered prior authorization company Humata Health is bringing Liz Fowler, former director of the Center for Medicare and Medicaid Innovation (CMMI), onto its board of directors.

The company said in an announcement that Fowler's experience at CMMI will support the team's efforts to accelerate Humata's mission to ensure patients get the care they need quickly. At CMMI, Fowler was tasked with rethinking reimbursement in Medicare and Medicaid to drive uptake of value-based care, providing critical insights she can bring to Humata.

Before her stint at CMMI, Fowler held leadership roles at the Commonwealth Fund and Johnson & Johnson. She currently serves as a distinguished scholar at the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health and the Carey Business School.

“Liz is a unicorn, a rare leader with deep experience in all realms of the healthcare ecosystem,” said Jeremy Friese, M.D., Humata’s co-founder and CEO, in the announcement. “She understands the inherent complexities of the health system, and her expertise will be instrumental as we work toward our mission of solving these challenges."

From her role driving innovation at CMMI to her track record of bipartisan work on federal health legislation, Liz’s experience will be an invaluable asset as we make AI-driven medical review a seamless process that ensures every patient receives the right care as quickly as possible," Friese said.

> Jeff Balser, M.D., Ph.D., is retiring from his roles as CEO of Vanderbilt Health and dean of Vanderbilt University School of Medicine at the end of 2026.

The academic system's board of directors has kicked of a national search for a successor who would hold both titles, according to the March 12 announcement.

A 1990 Vanderbilt graduate himself, Balser spent years as a cardiac anesthesiologist and surgical intensivist at Johns Hopkins before returning to Vanderbilt in 1998. He was named chair of the Department of Anesthesiology in 2001, dean of the medical school in 2008 and head of the Vanderbilt University Medical Center in 2009. He has served as dean and in the health system's top executive role for the past 17 years.

The organization has grown from a four-hospital system to an eight-hospital system under his tenure, during which its annual net revenue also grew from around $2 billion to nearly $9 billion, according to the announcement. Part of that time was an effort he led to legally and financially separate the medical center and its system from the university as a standalone nonprofit. The organization's cash reserves also have also grown from 50 days to more than 110, with total cash on hand plus investments now exceeding $3 billion.

> Greg Hoffman, chief financial officer of Providence, will retire in June, the organization announced.

Hoffman has been with the major nonprofit system for over a decade, and served as CFO for the past five years. His tenure in the role is marked by a multi-year financial turnaround that finally brought operations into the black during the quarter ended Sept. 30, 2025. The announcement of his retirement also pointed to his role striking up new strategic partnerships, resetting contracts with commercial payers and heading the implementation of an enterprise resource planning system.

Providence said it plans to conduct an internal and national search for Hoffman's replacement.

> Kevin Tabb, M.D., president and CEO of Beth Israel Lahey Health, has given notice to the system's board that he plans to step down after this year.

The physician executive was tapped as CEO of Beth Israel Deaconess in 2011, and stayed at the top through its merger with fellow Massachusetts provider Lahey Health in 2019. Today the organization spans 14 hospitals, employs 42,000 people and cares for 1.7 million patients annually.

Alongside navigating the merger and a pandemic, Tabb is credited with advancing initiatives around care access and connecting services delivered between its locations. Beth Israel Lahey also notched new partnerships: a teaching relationship with UMass Chan School of Medicine and an upcoming cancer collaboration with the Dana-Farber Cancer Institute.

Tabb will remain in his role while the system's board searches for his successor, a process expected to take about eight to 10 months, according to the announcement.

> Health tech company Stellar Health has added Raul Smith, a veteran of Elevance Health, as its chief financial officer.

Smith has 20 years of experience in healthcare leadership, according to an announcement, including six years as president CFO for Anthem's East Region. He has also served as CFO for Duo Health and Gold Kidney Health Plan.

Stellar said that Smith's experience in the health insurance industry will help the company build out its platform. Stellar supports payers in value-based care by making it easier for providers to engage with these complex environments.

In addition, he'll play a key role in driving the financial evolution of its risk-based portfolio, including the tech-enabled accountable care organization, shaco.

"Stellar is already setting the pace for value-based care, and Raul adds the institutional caliber to match our growing scale," said Michael Meng, CEO and cofounder of Stellar Health. She brings almost two decades of experience in Medicaid and long-term services and supports (LTSS) to the role, per an announcement from the insurer. Chinetti has held multiple roles within TennCare and most recently served as a staff vice president within Elevance Health's government unit, where she led a center of excellence in LTSS.

I look forward to working alongside TennCare, providers, advocates, and community leaders to strengthen rural and maternal health, enhance long-term services and supports, and deliver more coordinated, person-centered care that helps our members live healthier, more independent lives.”

> James Downing, M.D., will be stepping down from his role as president and CEO of St. Jude Children's Research Hospital near the end of 2026, the pediatric research hospital announced.

The physician executive has been with the organization for four decades, and has sat at its head for the past 12 years. Under his leadership St. Jude underwent the largest strategic expansion in its history, which included the completion of two separate strategic plans requiring nearly $20 billion of investment and over 2,300 new positions; major expansion to St. Jude's main campus; and greater international collaboration.

Prior to that he served as scientific director for eight years, a period in which he spearheaded the Pediatric Cancer Genome Project.

Downing plans to transition into a faculty role at St. Jude's Department of Global Pediatric Medicine after handing over the reins. The organization said a replacement is expected to be named over the summer.

“I’ve watched St. Jude transform the care of pediatric cancer around the world, and I’m grateful to have played a small part as its CEO," he said in the announcement. "It has been the honor of a lifetime to wake up every day and know that I have a purpose, to be a part of this workforce, and to change the outlook for children everywhere.”

>The Department of Health and Human Services announced several leadership changes as the agency looks to advance Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr.'s Make American Healthy Again agenda.

HHS said that Chris Klomp will step into the role of chief counselor at the agency, where he will "oversee all operations of the department." Klomp currently serves as the director of Medicare.

In addition, both Politico and the Washington Post have reported that Jim O'Neill, deputy secretary of HHS and the acting director of the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, will step down from both roles. Sources told the Post that O'Neill will be offered a position as an ambassador.

The Post reported that the shakeup comes amid significant controversy over the shift at HHS in vaccine policy and other hot-button topics. Kennedy is looking to shift talking points to more politically palatable topics, like healthy food, per the Post.

In addition to the new title for Klomp, Kennedy has tapped Kyle Diamantas and Grace Graham to serve as senior counselors for the Food and Drug Administration as well as John Brooks to step in as senior counselor for the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services.

HHS said that each of these three will also continue to hold their current roles in addition to supporting Kennedy's office.

"I am proud to elevate battle-tested, principled leaders onto my immediate team—individuals with the courage and experience to help us move faster and go further as we work to Make America Healthy Again," Kennedy said in the press release.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/providers/commonspirit-health-talks-hand-3-more-north-dakota-hospitals-altru-health-system" hreflang="en">CommonSpirit Health signs definitive agreement to sell 3 more N.D. hospitals to Altru Health System</a>

📅 2026-11-04  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
CommonSpirit Health signs definitive agreement to sell 3 more N.D. hospitals to Altru Health System" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

CommonSpirit Health has locked in a deal to sell three hospitals to Altru Health System, rural North Dakota nonprofit.

The deal is contingent on regulatory review and other customary closing conditions, but expected to close in early 2027. The pair's new definitive agreement, announced Wednesday, follows 10 months of due diligence following a letter of intent unveiled near the top of the year (see that story below). Financial details were not disclosed.

The to-be-sold facilities are CHI St. Alexius Health Bismarck, a multispecialty acute care medical center in the state’s capital, and two smaller critical access hospitals, CHI St. Alexius Health Turtle Lake and CHI St. Alexius Health Garrison.

They would join Altru's flagship acute care hospital, a critical access hospital it also purchased from CommonSpirit in March, a trauma center, a specialty hospital and 20 practices spread across the northern part of the state.

The organizations said there are no changes to patient care or day-to-day operations related to the deal. The hospitals will maintain their Catholic identity and mission.

"Through our work together over the years, we’ve developed tremendous confidence in Altru’s commitment to high-quality, compassionate care," Tim Bricker, president of CommonSpirit Health's Central Region, said in reference to prior partnerships with the  We believe Altru is the right partner to build on St. Alexius’ proud legacy, honor its Catholic mission and continue serving patients and families for generations to come.”

CommonSpirit is in the midst of a sweeping operational overhaul as it looks to bolster its financial performance ahead of imminent Medicaid cuts. Those efforts have opened the $42.4 billion system to a string of hospital divestitures, including its four handoffs to Altru as well as the sale of a four-hospital Ohio system to UPMC in September.

CommonSpirit Health and Altru Health System are considering a deal to transfer three North Dakota hospitals to the latter, the organizations announced Thursday.

The pair’s signed letter of intent outlines plans to evaluate a potential deal for the facilities, a process they said would run “the next several months” before a potential definitive agreement might be struck.

In the balance are CHI St. Alexius Health Bismarck, a multispecialty acute care medical center in the state’s capital, and two smaller critical access hospitals, CHI St. Alexius Health Turtle Lake and CHI St. Alexius Health Garrison. All three are Catholic facilities within CommonSpirit’s regional healthcare system CHI St. Alexius Health.

Potential financial terms of any deal for the hospitals were not disclosed, nor were any details on whether other assets tied to the hospitals, such as clinics, would be included.

“This is a natural next step in exploring how we can continue to support strong, sustainable healthcare for the region,” Tim Bricker, president of CommonSpirit Health’s central region, which includes CHI St. Alexius Health, said in the announcement.

CommonSpirit is a $40 billion nonprofit that counted 137 hospitals in two dozen states. It’s in the midst of an operating performance turnaround initiative and, at this month’s J.P. Morgan Healthcare Conference, teased “a couple” of portfolio divestitures to be announced in the next quarter as part of the effort.

Grand Forks, North Dakota-based Altru Health System is a smaller nonprofit, with just over $700 million of revenue in 2024 from its sole acute care hospital, a specialty hospital and 20 practice locations in northeast North Dakota and northwest Minnesota. It opened a new $500 million hospital in January 2025.

The news comes hot on the heels of another deal between the systems for ownership of CHI St. Alexius Health Devils Lake, another critical access hospital. That handoff was unveiled in December and is set to close March 1.

Both deals have been described as a boon for rural communities and an opportunity for Altru to better coordinate its local care.

“Altru has a long history of serving communities across North Dakota and northwest Minnesota, and our organizations share a deep respect for the people we serve and the responsibility that comes with delivering local healthcare,” Todd Forkel, CEO of Altru, said in the announcement. “We look forward to working more closely with CommonSpirit to strengthen access to care for patients and families well into the future.”

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/regulatory/what-does-blue-wave-mean-healthcare-policy" hreflang="en">What Democratic control of Congress means for healthcare in 2027</a>

📅 2026-11-04  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
What Democratic control of Congress means for healthcare in 2027" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Tuesday’s forecast-busting blue wave puts wind in the sails of Democrats who have promised a stark departure from Trump-era healthcare policy—though whether or not the shifts will be incremental or seismic hinges on intra-party debates and the president’s veto.

As of noon Wednesday, Democrats had flipped seven seats in the Senate and 21 in the House, setting the party up for comfortable majorities as unexpected battleground races continue to be tallied.

Healthcare issues were a major talking point through many midterm election campaigns. In battleground Senate races, for instance, Democrats spent $65 million on ads primarily focused on healthcare, more than twice that of their next-most prominent topic. Healthcare was overall the second-most prominent issue featured in ads (as measured by spend) across the two parties, behind a push on taxation topics from Republicans.

And within those ads: promises to deliver lower costs, greater coverage and federal oversight, starting Jan. 1, 2027.

The immediate legislative impact will be seen in the bills new chamber leaders bring to the floor.

A health policy agenda circulated in September by a working group under Rep. Hakeem Jeffries (D-N.Y.)—the House’s current minority leader and likely speaker in the next Congress—outlined immediate, “First 100 Hours” goals that include repeal of the Medicaid coverage and funding cuts of 2025’s H.R. 1 (the One Big Beautiful Bill Act) as well as restoration of enhanced Affordable Care Act premium tax credits that expired at the top of 2026 and vastly increased marketplace plan premiums.

Other 100-hours priorities outlined in the document include medical debt protections, removal of visa program fees for healthcare workers, an end to an AI prior authorization program in Traditional Medicare, restoration of withheld National Institutes of Health funding, a block on immigration enforcement operations in hospitals and guaranteed no-cost coverage of recommended vaccines regardless of changes in federal recommendations.

Additionally, the new majorities would install key Democratic policy heads to congressional committees responsible for advancing healthcare legislation and conducting oversight into government and private sector conduct. Among those new chairs would be Vermont’s Bernie Sanders, a vocal opponent of corporate interests in healthcare and the country’s chief Medicare for All advocate, who would chair the Senate Health, Education, Labor and Pensions (HELP) Committee; and Oregon’s Ron Wyden, who as Finance Committee ranking member recently pursued oversight of Medicaid stakeholders, endorsed Sanders’ Medicare for All bill, led calls to hold “Big Insurance” accountable for administrative roadblocks to care and summarized his midterms reaction with a single word: “Subpoenas.”

Underpinning the party’s legislative leaders are a slew of new-to-office healthcare advocates. Several of those elected to Congress have backgrounds as physicians or other COVID-era public health experience. Among the standouts are Ohio Governor-elect Amy Acton, a pediatrician who was director of the state’s Department of Health and resigned early in the pandemic in protest of state leadership; and Michigan Senator-elect Abdul El-Sayed, an epidemiologist and Detroit’s former health director, who has made progressive health policy goals like Medicare for All a cornerstone of his campaign.

For the public and the healthcare industry, the key question is how Democrats will pursue their healthcare agenda under a divided government and lame-duck president.

Molly Turco, who covers healthcare policy at TD Cowen Washington Research Group, wrote in a Wednesday policy note that “with Trump in the [White House], passage of major reforms are very unlikely and even medium-sized policies, like rollbacks of Medicaid [state-directed payment] cuts or restoring ACA subsidies, are an uphill battle. But the midterm results and catalysts like the debt ceiling give Ds leverage.”

Consultants at BRG, in a media roundtable late last week, generally agreed with the veto hurdle, but noted that a stymied Congress still has room to write and pass bills that could become new policy in a future majority, as was the case with Medicaid work requirements pushed by Republicans during the end of the Biden administration but ultimately codified last year.

There is still an avenue for dealmaking between the parties, analysts and advisors noted, such as on areas with general bipartisan agreement (think this year’s pharmacy benefit manager reforms, or other major pain points like physician pay or surprise billing arbitration reform) as part of must-pass spending packages slated for this December and the coming years. The president, for his part, told outlets late Tuesday night that he intends to work with Democrats to pass bills he believes will benefit the country.

PricewaterhouseCoopers, in a Wednesday policy note, said “targeted bipartisan legislation is most likely to move. Look for action on cybersecurity, AI, price transparency, modernizing drug development and prior authorization processes. Regardless of congressional gridlock, continued regulatory implementation will shape near-term business and compliance requirements.”

Turco’s note predicts a dual messaging strategy for congressional Democrats on affordability and accountability, with the latter involving “an aggressive corporate investigation and hearings strategy.”

The TD Cowen analyst also questioned whether Democrats’ midterm over-performance and gains by progressive candidates will embolden the party to pursue and campaign on “bold” healthcare reforms like Medicare for All ahead of 2028.

Wyden’s recent endorsement of Sanders’ reform bill “is a powerful signal that even long-tenured moderates are under pressure to have a vision that reimagines [healthcare],” Turco wrote, though “many other D members are worried about blowback from M4A support that has almost no chance of being a viable policy. These members are vocal about their concerns and want to work on more realistic policies like restoring the ACA and Medicaid, building on IRA negotiations, reducing premiums/ deductibles for employer-based coverage, adding more benefits to Medicare, etc.”

Investment banking firm William Blair’s equity research team, in an election day brief, noted that Democratic governorships bring immediate impact to the healthcare industry as states make strategic decisions on how to implement, or offset, the provisions of H.R. 1.

The limited avenues for swiftly rolling back that sweeping bill mean that industry players, including Medicare Advantage plans and margin-strapped hospitals, should begin preparing for its financial headwinds, consultants and health systems heads have said. But that doesn’t mean Democrats’ strong night has dashed all hopes for rollbacks or other tweaks to ease the pain.

“While the election has changed who controls Congress, it doesn't change the financial challenges hospitals will face in 2027,” Chris Van Gorder, president and CEO of nonprofit integrated health system Scripps Health, said in a statement reacting to midterm election results. “Democrats won't have the votes to simply reverse major federal health care cuts over a presidential veto. But Congress is far from powerless. Lawmakers can use their oversight and budget authority to press for changes in how those cuts are carried out, protect critical health care funding where possible and work across party lines to ease the growing financial pressures on hospitals and impact on patients."

Even if political gridlock takes hold, Tuesday’s results ensure that affordability will be center stage in Washington, analysts said, especially as consumers’ cost pressures will likely persist well through the 2028 election cycle.

“Even where legislation stalls, don’t dismiss the headlines,” PwC advised healthcare executives. “Democratic hearings, investigations and proposed bills offer insight into the policies taking shape for 2028, while growing pressure around affordability continues to push the healthcare market toward consumer expectations. Executives who look beyond near-term political conflict, plan for multiple policy scenarios and adapt to evolving consumer demands will be better positioned for potential healthcare reform after 2028.”

On the virtual care front, ATA Action, the advocacy-focused affiliate of the American Telemedicine Association, said the election presents an opportunity to build on strong bipartisan support for telehealth and virtual care at the federal and state levels. The group plans to continue working across party lines to make Medicare telehealth policies permanent, expand digital health access and remove regulatory barriers to virtual care, Kyle Zebley, CEO of the ATA and Executive Director of ATA Action, said in a statement.

Energy Drinks, Stimulant Supplements Linked to Higher Heart Risks in Young Adults

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Your hub for heart health management, insights, and solutions

Learn the basics to keeping your heart healthy

New research suggests that caffeinated energy drinks and stimulant supplements are associated with significantly higher risks of serious heart problems, including cardiac arrest and heart attack in young adults.

The findings, to be presented at the American Heart Association (AHA) Scientific Sessions 2026 November 6–9 in Chicago, are preliminary and can’t prove that energy drinks cause heart problems.

Still, the results highlight concerning cardiovascular effects of these popular products, particularly among adults under 35 who consume them regularly.

“These products are marketed as everyday pick-me-ups and sold next to soda and bottled water, so most people assume they carry no real risk,” study author Sai Samayamanthula, a fourth-year medical student at the University of Virginia School of Medicine in Charlottesville, VA, said in a news release.

“For many healthy people, if these products are consumed occasionally, the risk is probably small. However, individuals who consume energy drinks or other stimulant-containing supplements regularly can quickly exceed the recommended daily limit for caffeine, so it’s important for people to know that there may be a risk of cardiovascular harm with these products,” Samayamanthula continued.

Researchers from the University of Virginia School of Medicine analyzed 28,345 reports submitted to the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) between 2017 and 2025.

Of these, 854 involved caffeinated energy drinks or stimulant supplements, while the remaining reports involved non-stimulant products.

Compared with non-stimulant products, cardiac arrest was reported approximately 11 times more often with energy drinks and stimulant supplements. Heart attacks were reported 4.5 times more often, while ventricular arrhythmias and palpitations were reported about four times more frequently.

Nearly 45% of heart-related reports involved adults ages 18 to 34.

“Our findings support the case for more systematic safety monitoring of caffeine- and stimulant-containing products. In addition, healthcare professionals should remember to ask their younger patients who have palpitations, chest pain or fainting spells if they recently or regularly consume these products,” Samayamanthula said.

Gregory M. Marcus, MD, professor of medicine at the University of California, San Francisco School of Medicine, and chair of the 2026 AHA scientific statement on caffeine and cardiovascular disease, wasn’t involved in the study, but said the research “raises concerns that products with very high amounts of caffeine may be harmful to the cardiovascular system.”

“Although there are biological reasons why that connection could be possible, the study does not prove that caffeine products cause these events, because other factors may have influenced the results found in this analysis,” Marcus said in a news release.

“More research is needed to understand whether these stimulant products are truly responsible for the reported heart-related events.”

Cheng-Han Chen, MD, board certified interventional cardiologist and medical director of the Structural Heart Program at MemorialCare Saddleback Medical Center in Laguna Hills, CA, said the findings, although concerning, are not surprising. Chen wasn’t involved in the study.

“We know that energy drinks can have significant effects on the cardiovascular system, primarily because of their stimulant properties,” he told Healthline.

“Caffeine can increase blood pressure and heart rate, while other ingredients, such as guarana and taurine, may further contribute to cardiovascular stimulation and increased stress hormone levels. What is particularly concerning is the potential for these products to trigger serious complications, including abnormal heart rhythms, especially in people with underlying cardiovascular conditions.”

Chen agreed that occasional energy drink consumption is unlikely to have long-term consequences for heart health in most healthy individuals. However, he said that “frequent or excessive consumption can potentially increase the risk of hypertension and cardiac arrhythmias.”

An AHA scientific statement published in July 2026 notes that up to 400 milligrams of caffeine daily is generally considered safe for most healthy adults. That’s roughly equivalent to four or five 8-ounce cups of brewed coffee, depending on how it’s prepared.

However, some energy drinks contain up to three times as much caffeine per fluid ounce as brewed coffee.

Certain energy drinks may also contain stimulants, such as guarana, yerba mate, theobromine, synephrine, and yohimbine.

These ingredients may compound caffeine’s effects on the cardiovascular system.

“Caffeine stimulates the cardiovascular system, primarily by increasing sympathetic nervous system activity. This can cause the heart to beat faster, raise blood pressure, and increase the workload on the heart,” Chen said.

“Energy drinks may contain additional stimulants, such as guarana and taurine, which can further increase blood pressure and stress hormone levels. With excessive consumption, these effects can become more pronounced and potentially interfere with the heart’s normal electrical activity, increasing the risk of abnormal heart rhythms. People with underlying cardiovascular conditions, particularly hypertension or a predisposition to arrhythmias, may be especially vulnerable to these effects.”

Other research has echoed these concerns. A 2025 review suggests that high-volume energy drink consumption can prolong the heart’s QTc interval, increase blood pressure, and raise heart rate beyond the effects of caffeine alone.

“There is growing concern about the cardiovascular effects of energy drinks and stimulant-containing supplements, particularly among younger adults,” Chen said.

“One challenge is that younger individuals may not recognize that these products can have significant effects on their heart and blood pressure, especially when consumed frequently or in combination with other caffeinated products.”

Young adults accounted for a substantial portion of the FDA reports included in the study, highlighting potential cardiovascular concerns for this age group.

People with existing heart conditions, a history of irregular heart rhythms, or heightened caffeine sensitivity may face additional risks. Those with undiagnosed genetic heart conditions may also be vulnerable.

A 2024 study identified cases of sudden cardiac arrest occurring shortly after energy drink consumption, particularly among people with underlying inherited heart conditions.

“As a cardiologist, I am particularly concerned about people who may have underlying cardiovascular conditions that have not yet been diagnosed,” Chen said.

“Excessive stimulant intake can potentially trigger symptoms such as palpitations, chest discomfort, and lightheadedness. In susceptible individuals, it may also increase the risk of more serious complications, including abnormal heart rhythms.”

Additionally, combining energy drinks with strenuous exercise or alcohol may introduce additional cardiovascular risks.

The findings suggest an association between energy drinks, stimulant supplements, and reported cardiovascular events, but they are preliminary and do not establish cause and effect.

The FDA database also relied on voluntary adverse-event reports, meaning some events may never be reported, while others may receive disproportionate attention.

Researchers also do not yet know how many people consumed these products without experiencing any problems. Without this variable, the reports cannot establish actual risk.

Other factors, including underlying health conditions, medications, and lifestyle habits, may also influence the results. More systematic research and controlled clinical trials are needed.

For most healthy adults, consuming up to 400 milligrams of caffeine per day is generally considered safe.

However, Chen noted that individual tolerance for caffeine may vary, and people with underlying cardiovascular conditions, particularly high blood pressure or abnormal heart rhythms, may need to limit or avoid caffeine altogether.

“Energy drinks can vary considerably in their caffeine content and serving sizes,” Chen said.

“Depending on the product, a single can may contain anywhere from 80 to 300 milligrams of caffeine. This means that consuming even one or two energy drinks can put someone close to or above the recommended daily limit, particularly when combined with coffee, tea, or other caffeinated supplements.”

Seek emergency medical attention if you experience chest pain, particularly if it’s accompanied by shortness of breath, fainting, or other symptoms of a possible heart attack.

If you live with a heart condition, ask your doctor about your daily caffeine intake to determine whether you need to cut back.

According to Chen, warning signs of excessive stimulant intake may include:

“Depending on the severity of these symptoms, medical attention may be necessary,” he said.

Still, most people may not need to give up caffeine entirely. Monitoring your habits could help protect your heart health. This may include:

“I encourage people to read product labels carefully and be aware of their total daily caffeine consumption,” Chen said. “I particularly recommend that people with underlying cardiovascular conditions avoid energy drinks.”

Fortnite Account With Pirate Token Listed On eBay For $9,000

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

Update: In a statement sent to TheGamer, a representative from Epic Games has confirmed that the eBay listing attempting to sell a Fortnite account with a Pirate Token has been taken down. In a follow-up on Twitter, the Fortnite Status account notes that buying and selling accounts is considered a "scam" and that every player should have an equal opportunity to find a Pirate Token.

Original story: Fortnite’s Pirate Token has its players scouring the island in every match like hungry kids trying to find Wonka’s golden tickets. Most want the token so that they can claim everything in Fortnite’s store without spending a single V-Buck. One person, however, is hoping to make some real-life cash.

The Pirate Token is a new item added to Fortnite on Sunday as part of its new One Piece-themed Treasure Hunters season. The tokens are hidden in chests but incredibly hard to find. If you track one down, it can be used to unlock almost everything in the item store for two weeks.

To be clear, it’s not a case of having to choose what you like, or even all of those items returning to their virtual shelves after the two weeks have passed. The items are yours to keep, which is why one player claiming to have found a token is hoping someone who hasn’t will be willing to pay big money to take it off their hands.

eBay seller nich-12290 has listed their Pirate Token on the auction site, and you’ll have to pay them $9,000 if you want to make it yours. That’s the “buy it now” price - there’s a chance you can get the token for a lot less by simply placing a bid. The bidding starts at $1,500, and although no bids have been placed, the auction will run for another ten days, provided nobody swoops in and coughs up that $9,000 asking price.

That’s a lot of money for a Pirate Token, even when you take into account what you might get if you have one. It’s difficult to determine whether the token is actually worth $9,000. Some time-consuming math would suggest no, it’s not. It’s difficult to pin down an exact number, but if you were to buy everything in the item store right now, it would cost you around $1,000.

What’s available in the store changes every day, but it is rarely a complete overhaul. There will be items that stay in there for days, if not weeks at a time. The One Piece skins and cosmetics headlining the store line-up right now, for example. They’re not going anywhere for a while, which means there won’t be another $1,000 worth of new stuff in the store this time tomorrow.

Combine that with the likelihood that those hunting down a Pirate Token likely already have some of the stuff currently available in the Fortnite Store, and while possible, it’s incredibly unlikely that you’re going to get $9,000 worth of cosmetics out of it. The listing is for an entire Fortnite account, too, as the token is non-transferable, so you wouldn’t even be able to flaunt it as your own under your usual handle. The account also comes with 144 skins. Perhaps if they’re rare, that $9,000 price tag will be a little more justified. Probably not, though.

Healthier Salt Substitute May Prevent Millions of Deaths From Heart Disease

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

A simple swap for salt could spare millions of cardiovascular disease-related deaths, new research suggests.

In a study published in Nature Health on November 4, researchers at The George Institute for Global Health modeled the potential impact of 193 countries switching from regular table salt to potassium-enriched salt.

They found the switch could prevent nearly 3 million deaths and more than 10 million new cases of heart and kidney disease worldwide each year.

In the United States, this simple salt swap could prevent nearly 105,000 deaths and about 492,000 new cases a year, the researchers estimated.

About 130.6 million U.S. adults have cardiovascular disease, according to the American Heart Association (AHA).

Liping Huang, PhD, a senior data analyst in the Food Policy Division at The George Institute for Global Health and co-lead author of the study, told Healthline the team started with current blood pressure levels by sex, age, and country.

The researchers then estimated how replacing all salt with a salt substitute would change people’s sodium and potassium intake, blood pressure, and disease risk, she said.The team modeled a blend of 75% sodium chloride and 25% potassium chloride. The study estimated these yearly results worldwide:

The authors wrote that the model relied on data from a few years ago and on many assumptions, and that any large-scale switch would take years.

Potassium-enriched salt replaces some of the sodium chloride in regular table salt with potassium chloride, according to The George Institute.Asked where other salts like kosher salt and sea salt fit in, Huang said those salts may contain some trace elements “which has those health halos around them, but essentially they’re still primarily sodium chloride.”Huang noted that potassium-enriched salt tastes “about the same as a regular table salt.” She said it’s possible a small number of people with a sensitive palate could taste a difference.

Huang said that “it’s really hard for people to cut back their salt intake.”“But the easier way is just to replace part of the sodium chloride in our normal salt with potassium chloride, because increasing potassium intake also helps to reduce blood pressure,” she said.The two minerals play opposite roles in the body, according to Cheng-Han Chen, MD, a board certified interventional cardiologist and medical director of the Structural Heart Program at MemorialCare Saddleback Medical Center in Laguna Hills, CA. Chen wasn’t involved in the study.

“While sodium causes water retention and increased blood pressure, potassium will help the kidneys remove sodium from the body and lower blood pressure,” Chen told Healthline.Huang said she worked on an earlier trial of about 21,000 people in 600 rural villages in northern China in 2021.

People who used potassium-enriched salt had a 14% lower risk of stroke, a 13% lower risk of cardiovascular events, and a 12% lower risk of early death than those who used regular salt, according to a 2021 study in The New England Journal of Medicine that summarized the trial’s results.

The World Health Organization (WHO) endorsed potassium-enriched salt in a 2025 guideline, saying it can reduce blood pressure and the risk of cardiovascular disease.

The AHA said potassium-based salt substitutes can help lower blood pressure in some adults. The Food and Drug Administration (FDA) issued labeling guidance in 2020 allowing an alternate name for potassium chloride on ingredient labels to include the word “salt.”“We think that blood pressure begins to improve as soon as 1 week after cutting back on sodium intake. The full benefit typically takes up to 4 weeks,” Chen said.

“We generally recommend a daily sodium limit of 2,300 milligrams (mg), ideally under 1,500 mg a day. Most people consume over 3,000 mg of sodium a day, well above the recommended limit,” Chen said.The WHO recommends less than 2,000 mg of sodium per day and suggests at least 3,510 mg of potassium a day.In the United States, more than 70% of salt intake comes from processed and restaurant foods, according to the AHA.

Huang said that some people with advanced chronic kidney disease should avoid potassium-enriched salt.When the kidneys don’t work well, the body may not clear extra potassium efficiently, she said. That can lead to hyperkalemia, or high potassium levels in the blood, which can raise the risk of cardiac arrest, Huang said.Among people with stage 3 to stage 5 kidney disease, switching to salt substitutes could prevent about 750,000 deaths a year by lowering blood pressure and cause about 100,000 deaths a year from hyperkalemia, the researchers estimated.The AHA said that people with kidney disease or who take medications that affect potassium should check with a healthcare professional before using these products.

An estimated 37 million U.S. adults have chronic kidney disease, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC).

Potassium-enriched salt is available at supermarkets in the U.S. Many major U.S. grocery chains, including Kroger, Walmart, and Publix, carry two types of products, according to their websites:

Potassium-enriched salt typically costs about twice as much as regular salt, according to the study.Huang said she hoped people would see the results and start thinking of the switch as “a simple thing I can do for their own health.”

The top 10 goalscorers in Europe in the 2020s: Mbappe vs Haaland for top spot, Salah 5th…

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Which players have scored the most goals in Europe’s top five leagues in the 2020s?

The 2010s were dominated by Lionel Messi and Cristiano Ronaldo, but neither are playing in Europe anymore and didn’t get enough goals before they left to stay in the top 10 scorers of the decade.

There are plenty of familiar names in the current top 10 though, with the three leading scorers separated by just three goals.

Here are the 10 players with the most goals across the Premier League, La Liga, Serie A, Bundesliga and Ligue 1 since the turn of the decade.

Schick knocked ex-Aston Villa striker Ollie Watkins out of the top 10 in October.

He began the 2020s on loan at RB Leipzig from Roma, but went on to find his home at Leverkusen, where’s he’s now closing in on 100 league goals.

Alexander Isak is catching up with him, but Schick is just ahead for now.

After emerging in European football with a prolific spell at Gent, David joined Lille in the summer of 2020.

Over the next five seasons in Ligue 1, 87 goals by the Canada international followed.

He found things a bit tougher in Serie A after joining Juventus in 2025, only scoring six times from 36 league outings so far.

Juventus have sent him on loan to Atletico Madrid for this season and he scored on his La Liga debut.

Arguably the least household name here, Budimir plays for Osasuna and has only ever made it to the play-off rounds of a European competition (the 2023-24 Conference League).

But the 41-cap, seven-goal Croatia international has showcased his reliability in La Liga over the past few seasons, hitting double figures in 2020-21, 2023-24, 2024-25 and 2025-26.

Only Kylian Mbappe and Robert Lewandowski – spoiler alert, but those are two names you’ll be seeing again later – outscored Budimir in La Liga in the 2024-25 season. Then in 2025-26, only Mbappe and the relegated Mallorca frontman Vedat Muriqi scored more.

Now 35, Budimir might not have too much time left at the top level, but is under contract with Osasuna for the rest of the season.

Now 30 years old, Guirassy has played exclusively in France and Germany during his career so far.

He began the 2020s in Ligue 1 with Amiens, but suffered relegation at the end of the 2019-20 season before moving back into the top flight with Rennes.

Whilst with Rennes, he added another 19 Ligue 1 goals to his five from the second half of 2019-20.

A move to Germany with Stuttgart followed in 2022, leading to 39 Bundesliga goals in two years. He came one shy of reaching the same amount from his first two seasons with Borussia Dortmund.

Martinez has been with Inter Milan since the summer of 2018, which means his debut season in Serie A was the only one that didn’t span into any of the 2020s.

It was also the only one in which he has failed to hit double figures of goals. The Argentine striker even enjoyed three consecutive seasons with 20+ goals between 2021-22 and 2023-24, ranking as Serie A’s top scorer for the latter.

Martinez has often played as part of a strike partnership at Inter, but his longevity enables him to outrank colleagues past and present like Romelu Lukaku, Edin Dzeko and Marcus Thuram for goals since the start of the decade.

Salah left Liverpool in the summer for Trabzonspor, marking the end (for now) of his goal-getting in Europe’s top leagues.

He was extraordinary in front of goal after joining Liverpool in 2017, being able to boast 128 Premier League goals in the 2020s alone.

During that time, he claimed two Golden Boots, including from the 2024-25 season when Liverpool won the league.

He should be staying in this top 10 long after his departure from Liverpool thanks to the collection of goals he has already built up.

Lewandowski denied Erling Haaland the chance to win a Golden Boot in the Bundesliga while maintaining outlandish levels of form for Bayern Munich.

Lewandowski then began firing them in for Barcelona, where he moved in 2022. More than 80 La Liga goals followed.

Now at the age of 38, Lewandowski only scored 14 times in the league last season, but has proven wrong anyone who may have doubted his ability to keep scoring regularly at this stage of his career.

The winner of the European Golden Shoe in each of his last two seasons with Bayern, Lewandowski was La Liga’s top scorer in his first with Barcelona.

He is now playing outside of Europe for Chicago Fire in MLS.

Kane has 99 Bundesliga goals to his name from 97 games since joining Bayern Munich in 2023 and was top of this ranking by the end of last season.

Before his spell with Bayern, he enjoyed some prolific seasons towards the end of his time in the Premier League with Tottenham Hotspur, including 30 in his last season.

The 33-year-old is in the final season of his contract with Bayern but is showing no signs of slowing down, even breaking the 60-goal barrier in all competitions last season.

Haaland is still behind Salah in the Premier League scoring chart for the 2020s, but his exploits in the Bundesliga with Borussia Dortmund from the start of the decade place him 50-odd goals above the Liverpool legend here.

Haaland entered Europe’s top five leagues by joining Dortmund in January 2020 and proceeded to score 62 Bundesliga goals from 67 games prior to his move to Manchester City.

The big Norwegian striker was in no mood to slow his scoring rate in England, setting a new Premier League record for most goals in a 38-game season at the first time of asking with 36 (from just 35 appearances).

Haaland shattered through the landmark of 100 Premier League goals during the 2025-26 season and at the age of 26, should still have plenty more goals up his sleeve in the years to come.

Mbappe will most likely be going toe to toe with Haaland over the next few years to finish in top spot.

By the time he left PSG in 2024, Mbappe had become the French club’s all-time record scorer. A move to Real Madrid followed and he went on to score a Pichichi-worthy 31 goals in his first La Liga season.

Mbappe finished with 25 to his name in his second – again being La Liga’s top scorer – which meant he was recently able to pass a half-century of La Liga goals. He will be hungry for more (and silverware) over the coming seasons.

Dragon's Dogma 2 Is Getting Anime Crossover Updates Now, So Start Praying for Berserk

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Dragon's Dogma 2 players have had a damn good run over the last few months, and Capcom doesn't appear to be ceasing support for its open world RPG anytime soon.

Having released the excellent Dark Arisen expansion in October — and reviving the title's popularity in the process — the developer is starting to roll out surprise collaborations.

Available in the game right now, a Delicious in Dungeon (also known as Dungeon Meshi) crossover adds two new outfits based on the comedic (and deceptively dark) manga and anime property.

You've got until the 29th January 2027 to redeem said costumes, which are based on main characters Laios and Marcille. They look great, and they come with adventuring backpacks!

You can find the sets in your storage upon loading up your save.

The update itself weighs in at 7.8GB, and also features a whole bunch of minor bug fixes. The official patch notes are available on the Dragon's Dogma 2 website.

Of course, this sudden collaboration begs the question: what's next? It'd be a bit weird for Capcom to wheel out a single crossover and then never speak of Dragon's Dogma 2 again — and so the smart money's on there being additional updates in the future.

Needless to say, we need that Berserk collab. For those who may not know, the first Dragon's Dogma, all the way back on PS3, had awesome armour sets from the dark fantasy manga — but re-releases removed them.

It's no secret that Dragon's Dogma as a whole was greatly influenced by the late Kentaro Miura's work, and so reviving the crossover would be amazing to see.

How Revolut Cut Its AI Coding Bill 90% by Self-Hosting an Open-Weight Model

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 4 • Edited on Nov 4 • Originally published at neuraitool.com

When British fintech Revolut rolled out AI coding assistants to roughly 2,000 engineers, the bill did what AI bills always do: it exploded. Some engineers were spending $1,000 — even $10,000 — a month on AI tokens with little measurable return.

So Revolut did something almost no company of its size has tried: it ditched the frontier APIs as its default and moved its engineers onto a self-hosted, open-weight model. The result, per Revolut's own numbers, was a roughly 90% cut in AI coding inference costs — plus coding that got about 60% faster.

David Abitbol, who runs AI operations and infrastructure at Revolut, laid out the timeline in a talk at the AI Engineer conference in New York (posted 29 October 2026, reported 3 November):

Claude Code and OpenAI's Codex haven't vanished — they're now the "exception" list, reached for only when the self-hosted setup can't do the job. GLM is text-only, so UI work and the hardest problems still go to frontier models.

The most important line in this story: "cheap unlimited tokens, not smarter AI, is what speeds up coding." Engineers didn't get a better model than Claude Opus — they got a model they could use without thinking about the meter running. Before the switch, people were rationing AI because every API call carried a bill. Unlimited, flat-cost tokens changed the behaviour — and the behaviour drove the speed gain.

There's a compliance angle too: Revolut runs the weights on US-based infrastructure rather than through Z.ai's hosted endpoint in China. For a regulated bank, self-hosting is also how you keep data inside your own legal perimeter.

You won't self-host a 754B model — but the playbook scales down: open models for routine work, frontier for exceptions. Smaller open-weight models like DeepSeek V4.1 Flash are MIT-licensed and downloadable today, and hosted inference for open models undercuts frontier pricing. Revolut's story is the extreme version of a trend that already reaches small teams.

If you run an engineering team and your AI bill makes your CFO flinch, the Revolut playbook — self-host an open model for the everyday work, keep the frontier for the exceptions — is no longer a theory. It's a documented playbook with numbers attached.

This is a condensed explainer. The full breakdown — timeline, the numbers that matter, honest caveats, and FAQ — is on NeurAITool: Revolut Cut AI Coding Costs 90% With Open Models (2026).

Let your AI agent look at your server — on the same screen, over the same SSH, without ever handing it a shell

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Maintaining an old VPS with an AI agent goes like this, every time:

AI: I can't see the server. Could you show me the service status? You: (opens a terminal, runs it, pastes) AI: Thanks. Now the config file, please. You: (opens an SFTP client, downloads it, pastes)

The obvious fix is to give the agent a shell. The obvious problem is that a shell does everything, out of sight, and you find out what it did afterwards.

sshboard is an open-source desktop app built for the space in between. You and the agent look at the same server, on the same screen, over one SSH connection — and everything the agent does scrolls past in your window as it happens.

sshboard speaks MCP, so Claude Code (or any MCP client) can use it. What it does not have is a tool that takes an arbitrary string and runs it in a shell. Instead the agent gets:

Every one of those calls is shown in a band on your screen, before it runs. If the screen cannot confirm it showed the line, the call is refused.

Per connection, you list the directories the agent may write to. The default is empty: not one byte. Inside them it can upload files and create directories. Delete, rename, move, chmod, restart services, install packages — those simply do not exist for the agent. You, the person, are not restricted at all.

Install the app from the releases page (Windows x64 / ARM64, macOS Apple Silicon — not code-signed yet, so SmartScreen and Gatekeeper will ask once). Start it, then:

The relay reads the app's token itself, so it never lands in your shell history or in Claude Code's config. Register connections in the app (or import them from ~/.ssh/config), connect, and ask the agent what is wrong with the mail server.

No server to point it at yet? The repository has a throwaway Docker SSH server: sh tools/test-server/up.sh.

Not an autonomous agent that fixes servers while you sleep. Not a replacement for your SSH client or your SFTP client — those stay yours. Not a way to move off the old VPS: the point is that the old setup keeps working, with a second pair of eyes beside you.
