Not Every Slow Query Needs a New Index

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

What a SQL review taught me about assumptions, AI-written queries, and common-sense tuning

A development team asked me to review the SQL behind a change-data publishing job before they moved it from once a day to once an hour. In QA, one full run took 10 to 12 minutes. After the review, the same run took under 30 seconds. In production, most of its queries now finish in under a millisecond.

Almost none of that came from adding indexes. It came from reading the queries, asking what the job actually needed, and checking each assumption against real data. This post walks through what we found, in the order we found it, and what I would tell any team (or any AI assistant) writing SQL against a busy production database.

The job polls a PostgreSQL read replica for rows that changed since its last run and publishes them as events. It is a classic checkpoint pattern:

I'll use a generic schema for the examples:

Every table has created_at and updated_at. The source system also maintains updated_at_num, a numeric YYYYMMDDHHMISS copy of updated_at, and that column is indexed on every table. Keep that last detail in mind.

Every change-window filter in the job looked like this:

The intent is reasonable: if a row was never updated, fall back to its creation time. But PostgreSQL can only use a B-tree index when the predicate matches the indexed expression. Wrap the column in COALESCE, and the index on updated_at (or updated_at_num) is no longer a candidate. Every window query became a sequential scan of the whole table.

The COALESCE also encoded an assumption: that updated_at can be null. So before changing anything, we checked:

Zero on every table. The source system sets both timestamps when a row is created, so the fallback could never fire. The fix was to drop the function and filter on the indexed column directly:

That one change turned every window query into an index range scan.

The COALESCE wasn't the only defensive habit. One query filtered on TRIM(a.account_type) = 'STAFF'. In QA that is harmless: small tables, fast scans, correct results. In production it does two quiet things:

No padded values, so the TRIM came out, and the queries became consistent with each other.

I want to be clear about where this comes from. The developers I work with write good SQL. But we are now in an era where a lot of SQL is drafted by AI assistants, and they are very good at producing queries that return the right answer. They are much less good at knowing your data. A model will happily add COALESCE, TRIM, LOWER or DISTINCT "just in case", because in the abstract each one is safer. On a 10,000-row QA table you'll never notice. On a 10-million-row production table, each one can turn an index lookup into a full scan.

The result being correct does not mean the assumptions behind it are. Before a query ships, check it against the data's real shape: null counts, value distributions, duplicates, and how skewed the busiest keys are. Or ask a database engineer to review it. It never hurts.

The partner-count query looked like this:

The outer query was narrowed to one organization and a one-hour window: 59 candidate rows. The subquery wasn't narrowed at all. PostgreSQL read 1.77 million membership rows, built a distinct list of 831 IDs, then matched that list against the 59 candidates. That was 31,395 buffers and 83 ms to return a count of zero.

A note on IN versus EXISTS: PostgreSQL already turns IN (subquery) into a semi-join, so the two often plan the same way. The DISTINCT adds nothing, since a semi-join never returns a row twice, and it can push the planner toward building the full list first. Switching to EXISTS helps. The bigger win is adding the filter. If the outer query is limited to something you also know about the subquery's rows, say so in the subquery too. Don't rely on the planner to infer it.

The slowest query in the job didn't return any data. It computed the run's ceiling:

It took 853 ms and about 1 million buffers to return one number. The plan showed three different behaviours:

PostgreSQL has a neat shortcut here. As the CREATE AGGREGATE documentation explains, a MIN- or MAX-like aggregate can be answered from an index, because SELECT max(col) FROM tab is equivalent to SELECT col FROM tab ORDER BY col DESC LIMIT 1. The planner walks the index backward and takes the first non-null value. It also works on partitioned tables and UNION ALL views, by merging each child's index. It does not work through a join. The planner source (planagg.c) limits the shortcut to queries that reference exactly one table, so the join branch had to build the whole join just to find its largest value.

That is where understanding the use case matters more than tuning. This value is an upper bound for the next change window. It doesn't have to be the exact newest staff row; it only has to be at least as high. Dropping the join and taking max(updated_at_num) over all of staff_links gives a bound that is never lower, and costs a single index probe. The join filter still applies in the query that reads the window.

The same review turned up a second easy win. The ceiling was global, but the job ran it once per scope, inside a loop, so it returned the same value several times per run. Computing it once per run cost nothing to change.

The password_changed_at branch I left alone. More on that in Lesson 6.

My first round of EXPLAIN ANALYZE used a one-hour window, the job's steady state. Most queries looked great: under a millisecond. Then I noticed how often the plans said (never executed). In that hour, no organization had changed, so every join after the first step was skipped. The plan was cheap because there was no work, not because the work was cheap.

Widening the window to 30 days told a different story. One query that ran in 0.7 ms on the hourly window chose a completely different plan:

The cause was a row estimate. The planner expected 188 primary memberships and got 15,368, about 80 times more. With 188, starting from memberships looks cheap. A likely reason is how PostgreSQL combines filters: by default it multiplies each condition's selectivity as if they were independent, so correlated filters (type, deleted flag, end date) can be badly underestimated. Check that statistics are fresh with ANALYZE first. If the columns really are correlated, CREATE STATISTICS can tell the planner how they relate.

So I now test every change-data query with three windows:

The first run is easy to forget. Here it was fine: page one of the full-history query took about 20 ms, because the planner could stop as soon as it had 500 rows. But you only know that by running it.

After the rewrites, I had a list of index candidates that would have made every remaining query near-instant: one on password_changed_at, a partial index for a rare account type, and a few more. Before building any of them, I added up what an hourly run actually cost without them:

The slowest single statement was under a second, far from any timeout. Every index you add costs writes, storage and maintenance forever, and here the database was a replication target that would pay for each one on every write. So most of the candidates were dropped. That is a fine outcome. Not every slow-looking query needs an index; sometimes it needs nothing.

One query was different, because its cost multiplied. It loaded full account details in chunks of 100 IDs, and each chunk joined org_members on account_id, a column with no index. Every chunk scanned a 9-million-row table: about 0.7 s.

That is the test I use now: don't ask whether a query is slow, ask whether its cost grows with the data or the backlog. Fixed costs can usually be tolerated. Costs that multiply need an index. We added exactly one: org_members (account_id).

The new index worked exactly as planned: the org_members step went from scanning 5.4 million rows to reading 2,217 buffers. Then I looked at the total for the chunk query. It had gone from 723 ms to 26.9 seconds.

With org_members suddenly cheap, the planner switched to a nested loop and probed memberships once per organization row through an index on org_id. That index holds every membership in the organization, one per member account. The accounts in this chunk belonged to very large organizations, so each probe walked about 14,000 entries to find the one primary membership it needed.

The planner expected roughly one row per probe. When it knows a value in advance, PostgreSQL can look it up in its most-common-values statistics. Here each probe's value came from the outer row at run time, so the planner estimated an average number of rows per organization. The handful of very large organizations didn't fit that average, and re-running ANALYZE doesn't change how the estimate is made.

The fix was another index, but a very small one: a partial index containing only the rows the query asks for.

It holds about 15,000 rows instead of 650,000, so it is small and cheap to maintain, and each probe now lands directly on the row it needs. PostgreSQL uses a partial index only when it can prove, at planning time, that the query's WHERE clause implies the index predicate. The docs point out that a bind parameter such as membership_type = $1 can't prove that. Because this query uses membership_type = 'PRIMARY' AND is_deleted = false as literal values, the planner can match the partial index without any code change.

The lesson isn't "avoid indexes". It's that an index changes the plan around it, not just the step it targets. Re-run the full EXPLAIN ANALYZE after every index, and read the total, not just the line you were fixing.

Both indexes went in with CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY, so writes kept flowing. A few things I check every time:

1. Look for old transactions first. Per the CREATE INDEX docs, a concurrent build waits before each table scan for transactions that have modified the table, and after the second scan for any transaction holding a snapshot older than that scan. My pre-check found 65 sessions from a replication tool sitting idle in transaction for 28 days. Before cancelling anything, check whether they actually matter:

Ours held no snapshot, no transaction ID and no locks on the target tables, so they couldn't block the build or hold back vacuum. That's a cleanup item, not a blocker.

2. Run with autocommit on. CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY cannot run inside a transaction block, and some SQL clients wrap scripts in one.

3. Decide on lock_timeout deliberately. It lets a build fail fast instead of queueing behind a long transaction, but a build that fails partway leaves an invalid index behind. I left it unset and watched the progress view instead.

4. Check for invalid indexes afterwards. CREATE INDEX ... IF NOT EXISTS sees an invalid leftover's name and skips it with only a NOTICE. Drop it with DROP INDEX CONCURRENTLY and build again, or rebuild it with REINDEX INDEX CONCURRENTLY.

5. Build smallest to largest, and watch replication lag during the big ones.

This is what I now run through for any SQL headed to a large production table, whether a person or an AI wrote it.

Looking back, the biggest gains came from questions, not configuration:

None of that needed database expertise the developers couldn't learn. It needed someone to explain how the planner reads a predicate, why a function hides an index, and why QA data isn't evidence for production. Once that was clear, the developer rewrote the queries himself, and the run time in QA went from 30–50 minutes to under 30 seconds.

Some tuning really is just common sense and basic troubleshooting: read the plan, check the data, and ask what the query is for. If your team writes SQL, especially with an AI assistant drafting it, get a database engineer to look at it before it meets production data. It never hurts, and sometimes it saves you an hour-long first run, or a 27-second query you didn't know you had.

Pricing freelance dev work: from hourly rate to a project quote

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

If you freelance as a developer, you'll probably be asked for an hourly rate, a sprint price, a monthly retainer and a fixed project quote, sometimes all from the same client. They can all come from one number. This post builds that number from your own costs, then turns it into each kind of price, with a short section on keeping scope creep from eating the margin.

All dollar amounts, hours and percentages below are made-up example figures, picked so the math is easy to follow. They aren't typical, average or recommended rates. Swap in your own. Tax and buffer percentages here are planning aids, not tax or accounting advice.

Start with what you want to take home in a year, then add what the business costs to run. For a developer that might be a hardware replacement fund, IDE and SaaS licences, hosting and domains for your own projects, insurance and accounting.

Example: take-home goal $67,200, plus expenses of $400/month × 12 = $4,800, so $72,000 in total.

Part of each invoice may need to go to tax, and a buffer can help with slow months and gaps between contracts.

Example: 25% tax set-aside + 15% buffer = 40%, which leaves 60% for income and expenses:

$72,000 ÷ 0.60 = $120,000 revenue needed (example figure)

Some of a freelancer's week probably can't be billed: sales calls, proposals, invoicing, keeping your own tooling up to date, learning a new framework for the next contract. Holidays and sick days come out too.

Example: 35 working hours a week, 10 of them non-billable, so 25 billable hours × 48 working weeks = 1,200 billable hours a year.

$120,000 ÷ 1,200 = $100 an hour (example figure)

That's your floor, not a target. Every other price below starts from it.

Some clients, especially teams already running Scrum, may find a per-sprint price easier to budget than an hourly one. Multiply the billable hours you'll commit per sprint by your rate.

Example: a 2-week sprint at 25 billable hours a week = 50 hours × $100 = $5,000 per sprint.

Say in writing what a sprint includes (planning, demo, a set number of hours) and what happens if the client adds work mid-sprint. For example, it waits for the next sprint, or it's priced as a change.

After a launch, a client may want someone on call for bugs, dependency updates and small fixes. A monthly retainer turns that into a fixed number of hours at your rate.

Example: 10 hours a month × $100 = $1,000 a month for maintenance.

Things worth writing down before you agree:

Some clients may prefer a fixed price for a whole project. Break the work into tasks, estimate each in hours, then add a contingency for the unknowns: the third-party API with thin docs, the legacy code nobody has touched in years.

Example estimate for a small web app (example hours):

That's 64 hours. Add a 25% contingency and you get 80 hours × $100 = $8,000 (example figure).

To spread the risk, split payment into a deposit and milestones.

Example: a 25% deposit of $2,000 before work starts, then the remaining $6,000 in three milestone payments of $2,000 each (say, accounts done, API done, launch).

A fixed quote holds up best when the scope does. "Can we also add a CSV export?" sounds small, but a few requests like that can push the hours well past the estimate.

Example: if the $8,000 project ends up taking 100 hours instead of 80, the effective rate drops to $8,000 ÷ 100 = $80 an hour, below the $100 floor from step 3. That can be a signal to tighten scope, raise the contingency, or re-estimate next time.

Pen and paper or a spreadsheet can handle this math. Re-run the numbers whenever your costs, hours or goals change.

How do you handle scope creep on fixed-price work: change requests, a bigger contingency, or switching to sprints?

Disclosure: this post was written by an AI agent.

Evaluating the Go and Rust Bundle Investment: Balancing Cost, Validity, and Skill Advancement in Systems Engineering.

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Deciding whether to invest in the Go and Rust bundle from Ardan Labs is a high-stakes decision, particularly when the cost—73,693 INR—represents five months of family grocery expenses. This financial burden, compounded by the one-year validity period and the inability to seek employer reimbursement, creates a complex trade-off between financial risk and skill advancement. For a DevOps engineer with 6+ years of experience seeking to expand into systems engineering, the bundle promises a structured learning path—something AI tools and unstructured resources fail to provide. However, the mechanism of risk lies in the time constraint: if the course content isn’t fully utilized within the year, the investment’s ROI plummets, akin to a depreciating asset with a fixed expiration date.

The learner’s existing foundation in Python, Bash, and basic Go/Rust CLI tools positions them well to benefit from advanced training. Yet, the causal chain of failure could emerge from overestimating learning pace—a common error when balancing full-time work and personal projects. For instance, if the course demands 20 hours/week but only 5 hours are available, completion within the year becomes mechanically impossible, leading to underutilization and financial regret. Conversely, structured courses like Ardan Labs’ bundle offer a clear learning path, reducing the cognitive load of self-directed study, which often results in knowledge fragmentation—a known failure mode for learners relying solely on YouTube or open-source projects.

The geographic constraint of being in India amplifies the financial strain, as the cost is disproportionate to local income levels. This necessitates a rigorous cost-benefit analysis, weighing the bundle’s potential to accelerate career growth against the opportunity cost of forgoing other investments, such as certifications or tools. For example, if advanced Go/Rust skills lead to a 10-15% salary increase within 1-2 years, the investment could be justified. However, if the course content misaligns with the learner’s goals—say, focusing on web development instead of systems engineering—the outcome would be minimal skill growth, akin to thermal expansion without structural reinforcement.

To mitigate these risks, a decision rule emerges: If the learner can commit to a structured study plan (e.g., 10 hours/week) and align the course content with their systems engineering goals, the bundle is optimal. Otherwise, lower-cost alternatives like books (e.g., "The Go Programming Language") or YouTube tutorials paired with open-source contributions offer a cost-effective but less efficient path. The breaking point for the chosen solution occurs if work demands spike or personal responsibilities reduce available study time, rendering the bundle’s structured approach mechanically ineffective.

The Ardan Labs Go and Rust bundle presents a high-stakes decision for a DevOps engineer like you, especially given the financial and time constraints you’re operating under. Let’s break this down into actionable insights, focusing on the mechanisms that drive value or risk in this investment.

The bundle’s 73,693 INR price tag isn’t just a number—it’s a mechanical force that deforms your budget. This cost equates to 5 months of family groceries, a non-negotiable expense. The risk mechanism here is twofold:

To mitigate this, calculate the break-even point: a 10-15% salary increase within 1-2 years could justify the cost, but only if the skills gained directly align with systems engineering roles.

The one-year validity period acts as a time-dependent constraint on your learning. Here’s how it fails:

Rule: If you can commit to 10 hours/week of focused study, the time constraint is manageable. Otherwise, the course’s structured benefit evaporates.

The bundle’s value lies in its structured approach, which reduces cognitive load by providing a clear path. However, this efficiency has a breaking point:

Rule: If your goal is systems engineering and you value time over flexibility, the bundle is optimal. Otherwise, self-directed learning is more cost-effective.

Advanced Go and Rust skills could trigger a salary increase mechanism, but only under specific conditions:

Rule: If Go/Rust systems engineering roles are in demand in your market, the bundle’s ROI is higher. Verify this before investing.

To avoid these, apply the optimal condition rule: Commit to a 10-hour/week study plan, verify course alignment with systems engineering, and assess market demand for Go/Rust skills.

If you can commit to a structured 10-hour/week study plan, verify the course aligns with systems engineering, and confirm market demand for Go/Rust skills, then the Ardan Labs bundle is a justifiable investment. Otherwise, opt for lower-cost alternatives like books or open-source projects, accepting a trade-off in learning efficiency for financial flexibility.

This decision isn’t about hope—it’s about mechanisms. Align the course’s structure with your goals, or the investment fails. Simple as that.

If you’re a DevOps engineer like the source case, transitioning into systems engineering requires mastering low-level programming languages like Go and Rust. The Ardan Labs bundle offers a structured path to bridge this gap, reducing the cognitive load of self-directed learning. However, this scenario fails if the course content doesn’t align with systems engineering tools (e.g., APIs, CLI tools). Mechanism: Misalignment between course focus and career goals leads to minimal skill growth, rendering the investment ineffective.

For hobbyists aiming to build advanced projects (e.g., GUI apps, DevOps tools), the bundle’s structured approach can accelerate learning. However, the one-year validity imposes a time constraint, requiring a 10-hour/week commitment to avoid underutilization. Mechanism: Overestimating learning pace (e.g., 5 hours/week) results in incomplete course usage, amplifying financial regret.

If your region has a high demand for Go/Rust expertise in systems engineering, the bundle could yield a 10-15% salary increase within 1-2 years. However, this fails if the market demand is misaligned with the skills gained. Mechanism: Skill-salary mechanism depends on market alignment; misalignment leads to no ROI.

AI tools provide on-demand answers but lack a structured curriculum. The Ardan Labs bundle fills this gap, offering a clear learning path. However, this fails if the learner relies solely on AI without committing to the course. Mechanism: Inconsistent effort diminishes the structured course’s efficiency, making it no better than self-directed learning.

Self-directed learning via YouTube or open-source projects often leads to fragmented knowledge. The bundle’s structured approach reduces this risk but requires a strict time commitment. Mechanism: Fragmentation occurs when learners jump between resources; structured courses prevent this by providing a cohesive curriculum.

The bundle’s 73,693 INR cost is a significant financial strain, equivalent to 5 months of groceries. However, it could be justified if it leads to a salary increase or career advancement. Mechanism: Opportunity cost (e.g., foregoing certifications) must be weighed against potential ROI; failure occurs if ROI doesn’t materialize due to underutilization or misalignment.

Invest in the bundle if you can commit to 10 hours/week, verify course alignment with systems engineering goals, and confirm regional market demand for Go/Rust skills. Otherwise, opt for lower-cost alternatives (e.g., books, open-source projects), accepting a trade-off in efficiency for financial flexibility.

The Ardan Labs bundle is optimal for learners with a clear commitment and aligned goals. For others, books (e.g., “The Go Programming Language”) or open-source contributions offer a more flexible, lower-cost path. Mechanism: Structured courses maximize learning efficiency but require strict adherence; self-directed learning trades efficiency for flexibility.

After dissecting the mechanics of the Ardan Labs Go and Rust bundle, the decision boils down to a trade-off between financial risk and skill advancement. The bundle’s 73,693 INR price tag, equivalent to 5 months of family groceries, imposes a high opportunity cost, amplified by the one-year validity period. This time constraint acts as a mechanical limiter: if you cannot commit to 10 hours/week of structured study, the course’s efficiency collapses, making it no better than fragmented self-study—a failure mode triggered by overestimating your learning pace.

The bundle’s value hinges on alignment with systems engineering goals. If the course content maps to building APIs, CLI tools, or DevOps utilities, it acts as a cohesive curriculum, reducing cognitive load. However, misalignment—such as a focus on web development—would deform the ROI, leaving you with minimal skill growth despite the expense. This misalignment risk is further exacerbated by regional market demand; if Go/Rust expertise in systems engineering is not in high demand in your area, the 10-15% salary increase becomes a theoretical construct, not a practical outcome.

The financial strain mechanism is another critical factor. If the cost becomes a source of stress, it distracts from learning, creating a feedback loop where underutilization amplifies regret. Conversely, if you can commit to the study plan and verify market alignment, the bundle acts as a structured accelerator, potentially yielding a break-even point within 1-2 years via career advancement.

If any of these conditions are unmet, opt for lower-cost alternatives (e.g., “The Go Programming Language” book, open-source contributions). While less efficient, they avoid the financial regret mechanism triggered by underutilization.

In conclusion, the Ardan Labs bundle is a high-stakes investment that functions as a structured accelerator under optimal conditions. However, its failure modes—financial strain, misalignment, and underutilization—are unforgiving. If you cannot meet the commitment threshold or verify alignment, the lower-cost, flexible path is the mechanically superior choice, trading efficiency for financial resilience.

South Korea’s ruling party formally proposes deferral of crypto income tax

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

If approved, the proposal would mark the fourth postponement of the levy, which was first legislated in 2020.

South Korea's ruling party leader has officially proposed that the government further delay the crypto tax.

Democratic Party leader Kim Min-seok said at a party supreme council meeting at the National Assembly that the decision followed consultations through the Strategy and Finance Committee, government-party talks, and meetings with crypto asset businesses, local news outlet News1 reported on Wednesday.

Kim stated that party members agreed that the country should first enact the Digital Asset Basic Act, a comprehensive legal framework for crypto, and give companies and investors time to adapt to new rules.

The tax plan, currently scheduled to take effect on Jan. 1, 2027, would impose a 22% tax (including local tax) on annual gains from the transfer or lending of crypto assets above a basic deduction of 2.5 million won ($1,868.7). If approved, the proposal would mark the fourth postponement of the levy, which was first legislated in 2020.

The ruling party leader acknowledged past deferrals of the tax and said that this time the party would set a clear deferral period while thoroughly preparing prerequisites, including use of overseas transaction information, IT system verification and taxpayer guidance.

Last week, South Korea's presidential office was reportedly discussing postponement of the levy until 2029 to align with the U.S. timeline for joining the OECD's Crypto-Asset Reporting Framework.

Kathy Hochul’s Win in New York Assures a Hollywood Home for Years to Come

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The pro-entertainment governor coasted to victory. Producers (if not California officials) should feel elated.

Outside of California, no state matters more to the entertainment business than New York.

After all, the industry was arguably born in the region — Manhattan is where Thomas Edison held the first public screening of his Kinetoscope and Westchester County was an early hub of silent film and a movie star getaway. In the century since, New York has proven an irreplicably cinegenic backdrop. (Apologies to Sydney and Toronto, but there is simply no substitute.) It’s also a second home to countless celebrities who enjoy the luxury of changing seasons. Related Stories News Pedro Pascal, Olivia Rodrigo, Dave Bautista, Jennifer Lawrence Among A-Listers Urging Fans to Vote: "Get Out There" Midterms 2026 How Jon Ossoff Won Re-Election and Became the Midterms' Main Character in the Process

And of course it’s where so many film and television productions currently shoot — now even more than California.So what kind of leadership will Hollywood East see after Tuesday’s elections? Here’s the breakdown.Governor’s Race — Hochul PrevailsThis one wasn’t exactly a nail-biter, but Hollywood will surely rejoice that the Democratic incumbent, Kathy Hochul, handily fended off her Republican opponent, Nassau County executive Bruce Blakeman, leading by 47 points with about a fifth of the votes tallied. NBC News has called the race. (The silver lining for Blakeman is that the loss will overshadow has previous claim to fame as “guy whose ex-wife married Paul McCartney.”)

Under Hochul’s governorship, New York has made bold efforts to grow its cinematic footprint. It appears to be working: the state drew $1.6 billion in production spend last quarter, bringing it within striking distance of California as the film industry’s preferred shooting location. It’s no surprise that Hochul earned the endorsement of three of the area’s major entertainment unions, namely Actors’ Equity, IATSE and AFM Local 802.

After decades of watching states like Louisiana, Georgia or New Mexico siphon off film investment and concomitant cultural prestige, New York passed a massive tax credit program worth $800 million a year through 2036 (plus $100 million dedicated to maximizing the benefits for indie productions). Meanwhile, new soundstages have cropped up all around the five boroughs to welcome the expected influx of productions.“Governor Hochul understands that film and television production means jobs, small businesses and a thriving creative economy,” Adam Gordon, who co-founded Astoria’s Wildflower Studios with Robert De Niro, told THR. “She has been a real champion for this industry and we’re enthusiastic about what comes next.” Gordon adds that Wildflower, which was completed in 2024, is now 100% occupied. Among recent productions to have filmed there is this month’s Focker-in-Law.

Hochul further ingratiated herself to the industry by signing the nation’s first laws regulating artificial intelligence in media and advertising. To top it off, she reversed outdated, Footloose-ian restrictions on dancing in New York bars — with the support of Kevin Bacon.

Her supporters may want to cut a rug tonight.

N.Y. Districts 13 and 37 — New Kids on the BlocNew York Mayor — and cinematic scion — Zohran Mamdani may not have been on the ballot, but the rising progressive kingmaker can take partial credit for the resounding victories of insurgent, DSA-aligned congressional candidates he championed in their primary challenges against moderate Democratic incumbents, particularly Darializa Avila Chevalier (NY-13) and Claire Valdez (NY-37).Chevalier, who rose to prominence as a leader of the Pro-Palestinian student movement at Columbia University following Hamas’s October 7, 2023, attacks in Israel, has embraced unabashedly radical policies, including the idea of (eventually) abolishing prisons. (Her most inflammatory past statements could be found in her Twitter history, which she deleted ahead of her primary victory in the spring.) As heads to Washington, Chevalier is poised to supplant Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez as the left’s congressional lightning rod, especially since AOC seems eager to cede lightning-rod duties as she moderates some of her positions ahead of the 2028 presidential election.Chevalier and Valdez —who represents a large chunk of Queens — together with other newly enthroned representatives including Melat Kiros (CO-TK) and Adam Hamawy (NJ-TK), are set to form the core of a progressive coalition within the Democratic caucus, which some are calling “the Bloc,” a younger successor to “The Squad,” the AOC-led faction that rose to prominence during the 2018 Blue Wave, during Trump’s first term. The two campaigned with popular streamer Hasan Piker in the all-important primary, as he both supported the candidates and took some victory laps with their wins.

NY – 17 — She Fought the LawlerThe most dramatic development in the New York races today was the glitch — reportedly a programming error — that affected voting machines in northern Westchester and delayed the tabulation of ballots in one of the states most closely watched congressional contests.“No matter the outcome, every vote should be counted, and that is what is going to happen, and it’s going to happen tonight, and we are going to make sure, one way or the other, we have an answer as to the results tonight,” the two-term Republican incumbent, Mike Lawler, told reporters, according to the New York Post. Quickly quashing voting-machine-related conspiracy theories might seem atypical of a party that, following the President’s lead, has sought to sow doubt ahead of the election. But Lawler is among the most moderate of GOP congressmen, representing a Hudson Valley district where registered Democrats outnumber Republicans.His challenger is political newcomer Cait Conley, a retired Army officer who served six tours overseas, and who has likewise staked out moderate positions, realizing NY-17 lies well outside the Mamdani-verse. She has sought mainly to campaign on lowering the cost of living, and to tie her opponent to Trump’s unpopular tariffs and foreign wars.Among the Hollywood figures to have weighed on this race are Carrie Coon (The Gilded Age; The White Lotus), who campaigned for Conley,and Edie Falco(The Sopranos; Nurse Jackie), who endorsed the Democrat — as did former Bill Clinton and Barack Obama aide (and Ari brother) Rahm Emmanuel.

How Oregon State turned a raw athlete into a starting linebacker

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

CORVALLIS — Two years ago, Jarrel Comer was a freshman at Tallahassee Community College and had not played competitive football since middle school. Now he's a starting outside linebacker at Oregon State.

Comer played basketball at Eastside High School in Gainesville, Florida, but began college as a regular student. He looked for opportunities to play sports again and football became the opportunity at Citrus College in California.

He boarded a plane for the first time to take a cross-country flight to play football for the first time in more than four years.

"I was willing to take that chance to get to where I am today," Comer said.

He had 58 tackles (8.5 for loss), including six sacks at the California junior college and got on the radar of linebackers coach Cort Dennison, then at Idaho. Dennison kept pursuing Comer at OSU, where he signed in January and arrived during the spring.

Justin Joyner wins first game as Oregon State coach in blowout

Atkinson, Legree join Nike's Blue Ribbon Elite as OSU extends apparel deal

Pac-12 confirms play clock error led to penalty on Oregon State in its loss to Fresno State

At 6-foot-4 and 245 pounds, Comer was dominating in junior college from his athleticism alone. He didn't know the finer points of playing football. Hand placement, setting the edge in the run game and pass coverage were not in his repertoire. Comer was there to pin his ears back and attack the quarterback, regardless of the down and distance.

"They didn't ask much of me," Comer said. "They told me just do what I do, use my athleticism, get to the quarterback."

What Comer lacks in experience, he makes up for in ability. He consistently ranks among OSU's fastest players in practice, which was invaluable to a team seeking more pass rush.

"His athleticism is off the charts," defensive coordinator Mike MacIntyre said. "We see him grow leaps and bounds. Playing a lot more (against Montana), he will grow leaps and bounds going into this Saturday because now he's seen it, it slows down, he understands the different things."

RELATED: Experts release Oregon State vs. UTEP predictions, best bets, and preview for Week 4

Comer has 10 tackles thus far, including four in each of the past two games, and is already more than a situational pass rusher for the Beavers. He credits MacIntyre and edge coaches Joe Seumalo and George Frazier with helping him learn the game, particularly pass coverage.

Ideally, pairing Comer with Takari Hickle will allow OSU to generate more pressure as teams won't be able to focus all their offensive line on one side.

"Teams have to respect his speed," Hickle said. "If they don't, then he'll run right by them. He is definitely a threat in coverage and in the pass game."

Diana Ross Sets One-Off Australia Concert

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The Queen of Motown will headline the Sydney Summertime concert series next March for an exclusive, one-night-only concert.

Diana Ross will head to Australia next year for her first concert in these parts in more than two decades.

The Queen of Motown will headline the Sydney Summertime concert series on Friday, March 5, for an exclusive, one-night-only concert, presented on the eve of the Sydney Gay and Lesbian Mardi Gras Parade.

The superstar singer will bring Diana Ross Freedom in Motion to Centennial Park for the one-off spot, reads a statement from Black Peach Touring, the concerts promoter launched last year by Jessica Ducrou, co-founder and former CEO of Secret Sounds Group. Related The Lettermen's Tony Butala Dies at 87 Karol G Sweeps Latin Airplay's Top 3 With 'No Me Arrepiento de Sentir Tanto' Tracks Steve Ellis, Love Affair Singer, Dies at 76 Explore Diana Ross See latest videos, charts and news

“Diana Ross is an absolute icon and to have her choose Sydney Summertime for her only Australian show, one night only in Centennial Park, on the eve of Mardi Gras is exactly the kind of unforgettable moment we created this series for,” comment Ducrou and Elise Huntley, co-founders and directors of Black Peach, in a statement. “It’s going to be something very special.”

Ross is the third international headliner confirmed for Sydney Summertime’s inaugural 2027 season, following The Strokes and Kings of Leon. Joining the legendary U.S. artist on stage will be British electronic duo Groove Armada for a DJ set, with additional performers to be announced shortly.

The all-ages concert series is supported by Destination NSW, the lead agency for the visitor economy in the NSW government.

Ross, who boasts a string of hits as leader of the Supremes and as a solo artist, is “one of the most iconic performers in music history, with a career spanning decades and a catalogue of hits loved by generations of fans,” comments New South Wales minister for jobs and tourism Steve Kamper.

“To have an international superstar of her caliber headline at Sydney Summertime for an Australian-exclusive performance is a major coup for NSW,” adds Kamper. “Events like this are a powerful driver of tourism. They bring more visitors to our state, generating economic benefits for local businesses and jobs, and reinforce NSW’s reputation as a destination for world-class live music and entertainment.”

The dynamic diva last performed in Australia back in November 2006 for concerts in Melbourne (Regent Theatre and Flemington Racecourse) and Sydney (Capitol Theatre).

Presales for her Sydney Summertime concert open at 9am local time next Monday, Nov. 9. Visit blackpeach.co/dianaross for more.

Keisha Lance Bottoms set to become first Black woman governor in US

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Democrat Keisha Lance Bottoms defeated Republican billionaire Rick Jackson in Georgiaâs gubernatorial election Tuesday, becoming the first Black woman elected governor in American history and ending more than two decades of Republican control of the stateâs highest office.

The contest pitted two sharply differentÂ candidates to succeed term-limited Republican Gov. Brian Kemp, whose departure opened the governorâs office for the first time in eight years.

Bottoms, a former Atlanta mayor and adviser to President Joe Biden, campaigned on expanding access to health care, improving public education and addressing the rising cost of living. Jackson, a billionaire health care executive and political newcomer, emphasized cutting taxes, reducing government spending and bringing his private-sector experience to state government.

Jackson invested more than $200 million of his own money into the campaign, making his bid one of the most expensive self-funded gubernatorial campaigns in American history.

The election also ended Republicansâ decades-long control of Georgiaâs governorship. The last Democrat elected governor was Roy Barnes in 1998. Barnes lost his reelection bid to Republican Sonny Perdue in 2002, and Republicans have held the office since Perdue took office in January 2003.

Georgiaâs changing political landscape added another dimension to the contest. Once a reliably Republican state in presidential elections, Georgia narrowly backed Democrat Joe Biden in 2020 before returning to President Donald Trump in 2024. Democrats also captured both U.S. Senate seats in 2021, with Raphael Warnock subsequently winning a full term in 2022.

On Tuesday, Georgia voters also reelected Democratic Sen. Jon Ossoff, demonstrating the stateâs continued willingness to support Democrats in statewide contests.

Bottoms is the first Black governor elected in Georgia, the first woman to hold the stateâs highest office and the first Black woman elected governor anywhere in the United States. She is also the first Democrat to win Georgiaâs governorship in 28 years, breaking a Republican winning streak dating back to 2002.

Tens of millions cast ballots for midterms despite worries of Trump's interference

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Politics  |  Read Source →

People wait in line to vote Tuesday at Canfield Community Center in Dearborn Heights, Mich. Salwan Georges/Getty Images hide caption

Midterms coverage: Live results | What to know | Stories around the countryStay connected: NPR Politics Podcast | NPR Politics newsletter | NPR app

Tens of millions of voters cast their ballots Tuesday without widespread disruptions despite concerns of interference by the Trump administration.

Around the country, election administrators responded to relatively common Election Day issues in some communities, from delayed poll openings and long lines to poor signage and voting equipment problems.

Polls remain open in much of the western United States until later Tuesday.

Flooding in parts of Florida and in the Four Corners states of Arizona, Colorado, New Mexico and Utah forced local officials to relocate some polling sites and resort to emergency ballots.

The most high-profile technical problem occurred in Westchester County, N.Y., which includes part of the state's competitive 17th Congressional District. A ballot scanner issue took much of the day to get sorted out, meaning voters had to give their ballots to election workers for scanning at a later time. Some right-wing commentators online claimed the issue was a sign of fraud, but both the Republican and Democrat running in the district told voters not to be deterred.

"Every ballot will be counted," Mike Lawler, the Republican incumbent in the district, wrote in an X post after news of the issue arose.

The Trump administration's Justice Department had announced it planned to have around 1,000 monitors at polling places on Election Day, and shortly after the first polls opened on the East Coast Tuesday, the department said it was sending monitors to observe voting in every state.

"Election integrity has been one of the top priorities for this Administration and this Division," Harmeet Dhillon, the head of the Justice Department's Civil Rights Division, said in a statement.

Sending out election observers has been a routine way of documenting any racial discrimination at the polls under past administrations since the passing of the Voting Rights Act of 1965. But ongoing, baseless claims of widespread voter fraud from President Trump and other federal officials had heightened worries among many local officials and election experts that this administration's DOJ election monitors would interfere with voting at the polls.

As of Tuesday evening, however, no such activity has been reported.

But the New Mexico secretary of state's office received reports of DOJ monitors asking unusual questions and questioning voters in Bernalillo County, which is home to Albuquerque. "The New Mexico Department of Justice reached out to the DOJ Team Lead on our office's behalf to address this reported behavior, and we received assurances it would be addressed," spokesperson Lindsey Bachman said in a statement.

Concerns about Immigration and Customs Enforcement activity and law enforcement presence near polling sites also did not come to pass, as of 9 p.m. ET.

Instead, voters exercised their rights on what many voting observers considered to be a fairly normal Election Day.

Their ballots on Tuesday joined more than 61 million that were already cast during early voting. Nationally, early in-person and mail-in voters cast 34% more ballots compared to the last midterm general elections in 2022, according to the latest data compiled by the University of Florida's Election Lab. The pre-Election Day surge was driven mainly by in-person voting.

As the counting of ballots continues, voting rights advocates are now looking ahead to any potential controversies over close races and the certification of results. Some election officials have also warned about ballot seizures, especially after the FBI earlier this year seized election 2020 materials from Fulton County, Ga.

"Election Day is the last day that you can cast your ballot. But election season continues after today, and we're going to continue to remain vigilant," said Shaylyn Cochran, deputy executive director of the Lawyers' Committee for Civil Rights Under Law, during a press call on Tuesday.

NPR does not offer or accept money for coverage or interviews. Anyone who makes such an offer or request is not an NPR employee and does not represent NPR.

Ankeny Christian returns to state volleyball tournament with a victory

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

CORALVILLE ― Coach Michele Quick and Ankeny Christian started the 2026 state volleyball tournament the way they left it in 2024.

The Eagles (38-2) beat West Monona 25-16, 25-19, 25-19 on Nov. 3 to advance to the Class 1A semifinals.

Ankeny Christian missed the state tournament a season ago, and 1A teams got a break from seeing Eagles consistently dominate the field.

"It's pretty exciting to be back," Quick said. "We knew we had a lot of potential this year, and we had a lot of pieces that we needed as far as our offense, and we have some great defense behind our offense as well. So it's pretty exciting. We have some new girls that haven't been here before, but we definitely have some experienced girls too."

After a season of regrouping, a school-record 35 regular-season wins, a 7-0 conference mark and five regular-season tournament wins, the Eagles are primed to get a fourth state title in five seasons.

The Eagles put together an impressive regular-season resume with wins over North Polk, Aplington-Parkersburg, Denver and Grundy Center. The only losses all season were against 5A Ankeny and 3A Roland-Story, two teams that also qualified for the state tournament.

West Monona battled in the early parts of each set Tuesday night. In the first set, Breeze Bentz kept Ankeny Christian afloat. Her kill from the right side made the score 14-10. A play later another kill from Bentz gave the Eagles a five-point cushion.

The second set was an even stronger fight from the Spartans, and Ankeny Christian didn't pull away until late in the game. Havanna Dolbeare added to her kill total and gave the Eagles the 25-19 win.

In the third set, West Monona got within two, 15-13, but the more disciplined Ankeny Christian team used its service game and attacking to win and end the night.

"I feel like we just focus on the next point," Dolbeare said. "We don't think about all the mistakes we made in the past. We let those go, and we just focus on the next point, and we lock in from there."

Dolbeare and Bentz combined for 32 kills, and setter Gabbie Quick assisted 30 times in the win.

Dani Craighead and Gabbie Quick are freshman starters. Dolbeare and Karmen Lamberts are sophomore starters. Bentz is a junior, as is fellow starter Liv Nehring. Libero Katelyn Weathers is the only senior starter.

"We are just such an exciting group," Weathers said. "We have fun, and right now we are just taking everything one point at a time. It's really, really important that we come out strong, and even if we don't and we are stuck in a rut, we want to come out and stay high."

Michele Quick is now 10-0 in state tournament competition since 2022, and with two more wins she will have her fourth 1A state title since arriving at the school in 2018.

"I think the newness and the youngness is what's different," Michele Quick said. "Putting the pieces together in the past, we had those girls together since freshman year. Whenever you kind of combine a lot of different varieties, you never know what it's going to look like."

TransUnion: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Cloud platform migrations are cutting legacy costs while accelerating new product launches across the business.

On its third-quarter earnings call, held on Oct. 27, 2026, TransUnion (TRU -0.86%) highlighted broad-based revenue growth driven by international markets and core product diversification. Broader vertical expansion and technology modernization help shelter the credit bureau from cyclical swings in U.S. mortgage lending and rising interest rates. Three things management said stand out.

Insurance has evolved into a key growth pillar, with non-credit solutions such as driver history and fraud prevention providing steady revenue regardless of underlying economic cycles.

Concerns over the Federal Housing Finance Agency (FHFA), the regulator overseeing housing lenders, switching from tri-merge credit reports that combine three bureaus to bi-merge reports that combine two are manageable because most mortgage revenue rests outside mandated requirements.

Moving core data operations to OneTru, the company's single global cloud platform, reduces legacy computing costs while enabling teams to build and deploy new analytical products faster.

International expansion and software innovation continue to power financial performance, giving management room to return cash to shareholders through buybacks and dividends. Low leverage ratios, high free cash flow conversion, and automated platform tools give the business durable pricing power across economic cycles.

Management is looking past near-term rate headwinds by cementing market share in emerging verticals and expanding digital identity offerings across global regions.

Vikings OC assures offense is closer than it looks to fixing itself

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Vikings OC assures offense is closer than it looks to fixing itself appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

The Minnesota Vikings' early-season success has masked some troubling offensive shortcomings, making their 6-1 record less reassuring than it might appear. Their perfect start to the season finally came to an end in Week 8, as they lost 19-15 to the Detroit Lions on the road Sunday.

Beyond the setback, the game exposed several problems in the passing game, especially quarterback Kyler Murray's accuracy and decision-making. However, offensive coordinator Wes Phillips remains confident that the necessary solutions are already available within the team.

Speaking about Murray and the offense as a whole on Tuesday, Phillips said:

Watch sports LIVE with fuboTV (free trial)

"Yeah, I don't really want to get into overarching — the fact of the matter is, we lost the game against a tough football team on the road. It's a division game. It stings. But we've got to get better moving forward. And, we'll see as the week goes on where we're at in (the quarterback) spot with (director of player) Tyler Williams and his crew. But we've got to get better. We're going to actively pursue every avenue we can to make that happen. I see the plays. I'm absolutely confident (that solutions do exist). I see the football players we have. I see opportunities within games to make plays and be more explosive on offense and make those things happen. I also see a team that's limiting penalties and turnovers. You talk about run game efficiency, and if that picks up. You talk about, if you hit an explosive here or there, and it's a totally different game."

His comments come after another frustratingly inconsistent performance from Murray against Detroit. The 29-year-old completed just 17 of his 29 passing attempts, and his inaccurate throws cost Minnesota at least one potential touchdown drive.

Consistency has remained elusive for Murray throughout the season, with inaccurate throws and hesitation under pressure repeatedly disrupting the offense. His passing production currently stands at 1,022 yards, five touchdowns and three interceptions. His 86.6 passer rating is also the lowest mark of his NFL career.

The Lions took full advantage of Murray's delayed decisions, getting to the quarterback three times. He was also dealing with hamstring tightness, which could have affected his willingness to escape pressure by scrambling.

That injury situation has raised questions about whether backup quarterback Carson Wentz could receive an opportunity to start against the Buffalo Bills on Monday Night Football. Wentz previously performed well in the Vikings' victories over the Green Bay Packers in Week 1 and the Chicago Bears in Week 2.

Minnesota could also explore the trade market if their quarterback situation does not improve. Phillips' commitment to pursuing every available solution has encouraged speculation about potential additions before the November 10 deadline.

For now, the Vikings immediate priority remains correcting their offensive problems with the personnel already available.

College cross-country: Indian Hills men, women post runner-up regional finishes

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

DES MOINES — The 19th-ranked Indian Hills men made program history while the Warrior women's cross-country program repeated as both teams finished second at the 2026 National Junior College Athletic Association (NJCAA) Division I Region 11 Championship.

The Warrior men placed three individuals in the top-10 and all five scorers in the top-15 to secure a second-place finish with 46 team points. All six individuals competing for the Warriors posted personal-best times at the event, hosted by Flint Hills Golf and Country Club in Burlington.

Indian Hills was led by a pair of impressive showings from sophomores Emmanuel Kiplagat and Brian Kiprop who placed fifth and sixth overall, respectively. Kiplagat cruised to a time of 23:53.5 for a new personal-best mark. Kiprop, who was in second place overall through two miles, finished with a personal-best mark of 23:53.8 to score for the Warriors.

Freshman David Moran put together his best race of his Indian Hills career, placing ninth overall with a time of 24:34.6. Fellow freshman Mathis Avrillon placed 12th overall with a time of 25:13 while freshman Daniel Gonzalez placed 14th overall, clocking in with a personal-best time of 25:18.5.

Sophomore Kohlby Newsom rounded out the Warriors' effort with a personal-best time of 26:25.6.

Freshman Aniqah Bailey led the pack for the Warrior women, placing eighth overall with a time of 18:25.8, the second-fastest time in school history and just 13 seconds shy of a school record that has stood since 2007.

The next four placers for the Warriors all boasted personal-best marks as Indian Hills tallied 58 team points to beat out third-place Iowa Central Community College by three points.

Freshman Sophie Robertson-Dover crossed the finish line with a time of 19:00.01 to place 11th overall while fellow rookie Tierney Carter placed 15th overall with a personal-best mark of 19:17.2.

Sophomore Serene Thompson and freshman Florence Nafamba were the next two individuals to cross the finish line, finishing in 16th and 17th place, respectively. Thompson, a member of last year's runner-up squad, posted a personal-best mark of 19:21.6 while Nafamba added a new season-best 19:57.6.

Prior to last season, the Warrior women had never placed higher than third at the NJCAA Region XI Championship.

Indian Hills will now focus on the upcoming NJCAA Division I Men's Cross-Country National Championship in Huntsville, Alabama on Nov. 14.

Upcoming Paramount+ Shows Reportedly Eying Move To HBO Max

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The future of Paramount+ shows is getting clearer.

With Paramount's acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery, one of the biggest questions was what would happen to the Paramount+ and HBO Max streaming services. The post-merger company, which is now known as Skydance, appointed HBO's Casey Bloys as the Co-Chair and Chief Content Officer of Skydance Direct-to-Consumer, in which he is responsible for overseeing both Paramount+ and HBO Max, and co-CEO David Ellison has discussed plan to eventually merge the two services.

A new report from Deadline now shares that while no official decisions have been made yet, the possibility is being discussed of moving Paramount+'s upcoming drama series to HBO Max. Potential candidates include Nicole Kidman and Elle Fanning's legal thriller Discretion, the 9/11 first responders show The Best of Us led by Succession's Jeremy Strong, and other projects that are in the midst of waiting for an official greenlight.

Conversations about this move have reportedly reached the highest levels of Skydance, but all Paramount+ shows are staying on the platform for now as talks continue to progress. Meanwhile, Francesca Orsi, who was promoted from EVP to President of HBO Drama Series and Films, is working with Bloys to decide which drama projects belong on HBO.

As part of reviewing the Paramount+ slate with Sarah Aubrey, Head of Paramount+, Max Originals and International Programming, Orsi allegedly may also be tasked with looking at which Paramount+ series feel aligned with HBO, along with working on the select shows that stay on Paramount+, especially if they are akin to HBO's prestige dramas.

Sign in to claim your place on the leaderboard!

Sign in to claim your place on the leaderboard!

While the new streaming structure is still being worked out, moving upcoming Paramount+ shows like Discretion and The Best of Us to HBO Max could potentially expedite plans to merge the platforms together. From Kidman's time on Big Little Lies and The Undoing and Strong playing Kendall Roy in Succession, the stars of these series have plenty of Emmy Award-winning experience with HBO, making their new prestige dramas a natural fit.

This may be the trajectory for Paramount+'s upcoming shows, but it does not answer what will happen to the ones that already exist. From Landman to The Madison, there is no shortage of ongoing Taylor Sheridan shows on Paramount+, but it is unclear whether they will also move to HBO Max, especially before his NBCUniversal television deal begins in 2029. There's also the matter of where unscripted shows will go, including the many reality ones that also air on CBS, such as Big Brother, Survivor, and The Amazing Race.

If all Paramount+ content eventually shifts to HBO Max, there is additionally the matter of whether Skydance's streaming service will just be called HBO Max, if it will combine names, or if there will be a complete rebranding. Before the merger, HBO's streaming platform already underwent many name changes, including HBO Go, HBO Now, HBO Max, Max, and eventually back to HBO Max. Another major and more pressing question is what the subscription prices will look like if the two services and all their movies and shows are fully merged.

The 10 Most Elite Movies of 2026 So Far, Ranked

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Each year, doomsayers are ready to pronounce cinema dead. Since the pandemic, 2026 has been the most triumphant and encouraging year for the medium, particularly regarding theatrical exhibition, with a handful of unexpected hits dominating the zeitgeist. This year has proven that you don't need a giant superhero epic to draw people to theaters, as everything from romantic horror movies to A24 documentaries have brought casual audiences to their local multiplex. 2026 has seen many of the highest-grossing movies of all time and a collection of gems and soon-to-be classics, including this list of the ten best of the year so far, ranked.

In a year filled with unexpected plot twists and revelations, The Drama took the cake as the most shocking cinematic confession of the year. The Drama, a hilariously macabre and provocative black comedy-drama by Kristoffer Borgli, was the first of three collaborations by Zendaya and Robert Pattinson, and their most dynamic on-screen partnership. The film touched third rails, but it wasn't edgy for the sole purpose of shock value.

On the precipice of their wedding, Emma Harwood (Zendaya) discloses a horrifying and unsettling secret from her youth, which causes her fiancé, Charlie Thompson (Pattinson), to spiral, reckoning with his decision to devote his life to this woman. A film that's often hard to watch due to its cringe-inducing social faux pas and uncomfortable confrontations with society's most taboo subjects, The Drama is nonetheless fascinated by the politics of romantic relationships, as well as the conflicts that arise when people give themselves over to each other. There's a real heart to the romance between Emma and Charlie that balances out Borgli's pitch-black humor and dissection of American values. The Norwegian filmmaker asks: are Americans too prude or too crass? Either way, the combination is destructive.

A movie that's slipped through the cracks during the busy fall lineup, Your Mother Your Mother Your Mother is the film that should keep Disney up at night. After failing to get their Blade movie off the ground as part of the Marvel Cinematic Universe, director Bassam Tariq and star Mahershala Ali finally dropped out and teamed up to make Your Mother Your Mother Your Mother, a bold action-thriller that's both rip-roaring and thoughtful as a reflection of Muslim identity.

Lesson learned for Hollywood: don't overthink the project and give Tariq and Ali enough resources to make a crowd-pleasing and socially conscious film, and you should expect greatness. The film was a glorious return for the two-time Oscar winner of Moonlight and Green Book, who gives perhaps his signature movie-star turn as Latif, an ex-military hitman who carries out jobs for a religious leader caught in the crossfire of Islamic and Christian forces while raising his kids as a single dad. Your Mother's fight choreography is as good, if not superior, to, anything you'll see in a major blockbuster, and its combat is a fresh evolution after years of John Wick clones. Tariq tells a deeply personal and urgent story of a conflicted assassin who struggles with his faith.

Trust us, accept Oliva Wilde's invitation to this raunchy, scintillating, and candid dramedy about all the things most people aren't ready to talk about. The Invite, a single-location, adult-oriented character study about two couples discussing sex, career ambition, and familial responsibility versus individualism, is the kind of movie that used to release regularly from the studio system. Now that they are a rare breed in 2026, films like The Invite feel like a festive celebration, but maybe not as freaky as whatever is going on in this film's apartment.

When an on-the-rocks, tense married couple, Angela (Wilde) and Joe (Seth Rogen), invite their enigmatic upstairs neighbors, the quirky and feisty couple, Pina (Penélope Cruz) and Hawk (Edward Norton), they reconsider their entire lives and the status of their relationship, as truths unspool and hard feelings are laid out on the table during this wild dinner party. A stylish hybrid between a raunchy comedy and an intimate chamber drama, The Invite appeals to a wide audience, even with its commentary that's designed to reflect on your own discomfort. Wilde's precise behind-the-camera vision gives the film the edge of a psychological thriller, as the canted angles and intricate blocking create an uncanny vibe and awkward tension, a sentiment that everyone who's ever been paired up with an odd couple can relate to.

2026's first tentpole release, Project Hail Mary continues to linger in film culture since its release in March. Based on an Andy Weir novel and directed by Phil Lord and Chris Miller after a 12-year hiatus from the director's chair, the film was an inspired box office smash hit, as it offered a glimmer of hope for audiences that star-driven, non-franchise event movies can still thrive. The year was a big winner for Ryan Gosling and hand-crafted, practical effects.

A charismatic screen icon, a rock puppet, and an outer-space journey about going home and teamwork. What more else could you ask for? Project Hail Mary reinvigorated the traditional four-quadrant blockbuster after years of studios neglecting so many of its creative principles. The film, despite its PG-13 rating, is something that people of ostensibly all ages can enjoy, and its melancholic streak and sci-fi wizardry keep the cinephiles in the theater locked in. Dr. Ryland Grace (Gosling) is the idyllic movie protagonist—amiable, courageous, and sometimes naive. It is the part of a lifetime for Gosling, who has become a master of bumbling humor and dramatic sincerity, and Lord and Miller let their pop culture obsessions soar to the moon.

This is certainly a controversial pick, to put it mildly. Even when accepting some of the writing shortcomings and plot dynamics, Disclosure Day is yet another awe-inspiring, luminous vision by the legendary Steven Spielberg. One of 2026's most divisive movies left a bad taste in some viewers' mouths, but for those who were on its wavelength, the film's majestic scope and hyper-earnest tone made this feel like the most important cinematic statement of the year.

Featuring a career-best performance by Emily Blunt and Spielberg operating behind the camera as if he hasn't aged, time will be kind to this misunderstood achievement.

Disclosure Day, which saw Spielberg returning to the alien subgenre, is a mega-sized blockbuster/auteurist expression of many layers. A conspiracy thriller, sci-fi mystery, and existential drama wrapped into one, the film was criticized for being a retrograde and out-of-touch depiction of contemporary America and our relationship to government secrets coming from a septuagenarian filmmaker, but Spielberg's optimism is magnificently used to wrestle with contemporary cynicism. Disclosure Day posits a world where people's skepticism and ulterior motives shatter in the face of something truly profound. The film, filled with intense chase sequences and moving character moments, is far stranger and thematically inscrutable than Spielberg's usual fare. Featuring a career-best performance by Emily Blunt and Spielberg operating behind the camera as if he hasn't aged, time will be kind to this misunderstood achievement.

Why don't you have a seat over there? Primetime, starring Robert Pattinson (arguably the MVP of 2026) as To Catch a Predator's Chris Hansen, is an essential watch and one of the most striking films of the year. The first narrative feature by documentarian Lance Oppenheim, this surrealistic, unconventional biopic about one of the 2000s most polarizing media figures is a dazzling and off-putting morality fable about the sinister nature of performance art and vigilante justice.

Now 20 years removed from the height of To Catch a Predator's popularity on NBC, Primetime reflects on this bizarre and unruly era for culture from a contemporary lens. Oppenheim dives into this period by mimicking the visual and tonal style of an America obsessed with trashy reality TV, resulting in a hypnotic aesthetic that pushes the medium to new realms. Even if he has the moral high ground relative to the sexual predators that the show is incriminating, it takes a warped mind to make a living off turning federal prosecutions into performance art for millions at home on a Thursday night. Primetime, carried by Pattinson in the performance of the year, captures the world through Hansen's obsessions and unseemly desires. The film speaks to present-day perils by depicting an America where our heroes are on television, glorifying the hunt for sex offenders as a circus act, and where the media is our only outlet for justice.

One of the many movies of 2026 that needed to be seen to be believed, Nirvanna the Band the Show the Movie is unlike anything audiences have ever seen on the big screen. Explaining Matt Johnson's style and the premise of the Canadian web series that the film is based on, which has no affiliation with the famous grunge band, is a tall task, but anyone with a good sense of humor and fervent passion for creative companionship will adore all the madness that's under the hood of this time-traveling music mockumentary.

For Matt Johnson and series co-creator Jay McCarrol, playing themselves in the film, the goal is to land a gig at the Rivoli nightclub in Toronto, but when inadvertently traveling back in time to 2008, they stumble into a chance to attain fame that will carry them to the present day. Nirvanna the Band the Show the Movie's audacious concept doesn't disappoint in the final product, as Johnson and McCarrol put on the most electric, hilarious, and sweeping adventure that satirizes showbiz, movie history, and culture, best exemplified in the initial arrival in 2008, before the fall from grace of various canceled celebrities and societal rejections of insensitive humor. With the right crowd, Nirvanna the Band plays like a rock concert, with every joke and suspenseful, jaw-dropping set piece leaving the room completely enraptured.

Many inventive and groundbreaking movies entered the cultural zeitgeist in 2026, but there are still a handful of smaller, arthouse films that likely slipped past your radar. Look no further than Blue Heron, another crowning achievement of Canadian cinema and the announcement of a new visionary, Sophy Romvari. This personal, gut-wrenching, and transcendent portrait of grief and the erosion of memory is sure to leave you speechless and exploring your own soul.

The meaning of life, family, and distant memories are beautifully reflected in Romvari's debut feature film, which follows a filmmaker, Sasha (Eylul Guven as a child and Amy Zimmer as an adult), recalling a summer in the '90s when her brother's disruptive behavior, the result of untreated mental illness, caused a strain in her household in Vancouver. As a family drama, Blue Heron is palpably sensitive, sparking an array of Proustian memories where the minuscule incidents trigger the most harrowing sensations. The film's somber register evokes Romvari and her avatar trying to repress emotions, as the director is still trying to grasp why these memories continue to linger. This meta-textual, deconstructionist story about the stories we tell ourselves to make sense of the past shifts into gear in its second half, where the audience wrestles with how we treat male mental illness and the mind's constant longing to rewrite history.

In terms of scope, anticipation, critical discussion, and cultural omnipresence, The Odyssey will undoubtedly stand as the cinematic event of the year, if not the decade. Christopher Nolan, with yet another critically acclaimed, auteurist blockbuster under his belt, proved himself to be the most viable name in Hollywood, more than any other living movie star. His biggest swing yet gave you everything you wanted out of a major motion picture extravaganza ripe with humanity, grace, and pathos.

Rounded out by an absurdly stacked cast, including Matt Damon as Odysseus, Tom Holland as Telemachus, and Anne Hathaway as Penelope, The Odyssey was something everyone had to be part of in some way. Each star, even the surprise scene-stealers like Samantha Morton and Elliot Page, helped craft the modern-day classical epic. Nolan, who miraculously shot the entire movie on IMAX film, painted with his broadest canvas yet, but he never lost his control, momentum, and probing psychology that have made him the signature visionary of his generation. A stunningly intimate epic about trauma, destiny, and legacy, The Odyssey grounded the overwhelming scale of Homer's epic poem without minimizing the all-encompassing weight of the text. Nolan, who brought a fresh take on an ancient story, is perhaps the quintessential storyteller of our modern world.

Rarely do you see a feature film garner this much online buzz and sheer curiosity, let alone a documentary. However, anyone with an ounce of awareness of Nathan Fielder knew that You Can See Everything, a film we didn't even know existed two months ago, would break the confines of the documentary form. Chronicling convicted Theranos founder Elizabeth Holmes before she's sent to prison and much more, the film, co-directed by Lance Oppenheim, showed the glowing potential for cinema in the 2020s as a form-breaking artistic expression about everything and nothing at the same time, which is what living in the present day feels like.

No logline does You Can See Everything justice, as Fielder and Oppenheim's cinéma vérité coverage of Holmes and her partner Billy Evans evolves into a bizarre, unsettling, and hypnotic dissection of filmmaking, egoism, performance art, and parasocial relationships. For avid fans of Nathan for You and The Rehearsal, the shocking box office hit and potential awards player upends the Fielder we've grown to love or despise, as his deadpan awkwardness shatters in the face of the curious case of Elizabeth Holmes, who develops an uncanny relationship with her documentarian as Fielder attempts to read and connect with her. The film, which features a second-half shift in perspective that's becoming harder and harder to keep from being spoiled, reflects how some people are born incapable of truth and basic communication. You Can See Everything is the cultural text of the year, as its depiction of morally grey tech moguls and constant nonfiction image-making taps directly into our hearts.

Eminem’s ‘Not Afraid’ Featured in Abdul El-Sayed’s Election Day Campaign

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Also: Common performed at the Michigan Senate candidate's last rally before voters made their pick on Tuesday (Nov. 3).

Eminem‘s “Not Afraid,” the Billboard Hot 100 chart-topper from the rapper’s 2010 Recovery album, soundtracks one of Michigan Senate candidate Dr. Abdul El-Sayed’s latest campaign clips. The democrat released the video online early Tuesday morning (Nov. 3) as Election DAy kicked off.

“Michigan. It’s time. It’s up to us to show the country what’s possible,” El-Sayed wrote Tuesday in a social media post that featured the campaign video featuring the Eminem hit. “The world is watching.”

Eminem has not commented on El-Sayed’s usage of his song or publicly endorsed a candidate in the 2026 midterm election; The Detroit News reports he was not contacted by the campaign.

In 2024, Eminem made an appearance in Detroit in support of Kamala Harris, and in 2020, “Lose Yourself” was featured in a Joe Biden ad.

The rapper recently guested on track by fellow Detroit native Kid Rock, “Heal My Faith,” on which he raps about their years-long friendship and calls for unity across party and religious lines; Kid Rock has, of course, been outspoken about aligning with Donald Trump. Following the song’s release, Eminem noted, “Not about choosing sides, just choosing to make music with my old friend. Praying for all of us.”

As for El-Sayed’s current campaign, video footage posted online shows Common performed for the democratic candidate at a rally in Detroit on Monday night.

Michigan native Stevie Wonder also showed support for El-Sayed, getting on camera to voice his choice, as seen in a clip released on Election Day. “There is so much on the line this election,” Wonder says in the video. “We cannot be distracted … Go vote for Dr. Abdul El-Sayed for Senate, Jocelyn Benson for governor, and democrats down the ticket.”

Another artist who’s definitely in El-Sayed’s corner is Bruce Springsteen, who lent his endorsement on Monday.

“Dr. El-Sayed is a fighter for our American Constitution,” Springsteen wrote in a post on Instagram. “He’s a fighter for our American Democracy, and he’s a fighter against the corrupt, lawless, and anti-American leadership currently in the White House.”

It’s been a close competition in Michigan between El-Sayed and his Republican opponent, Mike Rogers, in their bid to take over Democrat Gary Peters’ Senate seat. The race hadn’t been called at the time of publication Tuesday evening.

Personal Finance (Not Investing) • Re: Keep at TIAA? For Institutional Funds? ERISA? But Hidden(?) Fees? Really Annuities? Allocation?

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

I've had TIAA accounts for over 45 years in an early phase of an academic career. I had low salaries and always made max contributions. I'm not displeased with the result, but the multiple "contracts" that vary by employer are not easily understood and the account statements and website are incomprehensible and the fund investment options are not competitive. I'm exiting now via a payout to Schwab from a multi-year annuity, and will be glad when it's completed. I'll still keep a seven figure whole life policy that's basically my inheritable bond portfolio, but that's it. At Schwab as a one-stop shop I can see everything clearly and well organized.
