Nike vs. Procter & Gamble: Which Consumer Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Nike trades at a premium valuation despite slowing growth, while P&G's defensive portfolio offers a contrasting risk-return profile.

Investors often weigh the potential of iconic growth brands against the stability of essential household names. Deciding between Nike (NKE -1.64%) and Procter & Gamble (PG -0.92%) means choosing between athletic innovation and daily necessity.

Nike serves the athletic world with footwear and apparel, leveraging a massive digital presence to reach global fans. Procter & Gamble offers a defensive shield with brands like Tide and Gillette. These companies represent different strategies for long-term growth and capital preservation in a shifting economic landscape.

Nike designs and sells athletic footwear, apparel, and equipment across the globe through its own digital channels and a network of wholesale partners. The company operates primarily in the retail stocks space, focusing on high-performance gear and lifestyle products. In its latest annual report, filed for the period ending May 31, 2026, the company noted that its three largest U.S. customers accounted for approximately 29% of sales. Customer concentration like this adds a layer of risk to the business.

In FY 2026, revenue reached nearly $46.4 billion. This performance follows a period of higher sales in earlier years, as the company reported approximately $51.4 billion in revenue during FY 2024. Net income for the most recent year was $3.1 billion.

Procter & Gamble manufactures branded consumer packaged goods across diverse categories, including Fabric & Home Care, Baby Care, and Beauty. The company sells to mass merchandisers, grocery stores, and drug stores, reaching billions of consumers worldwide. Walmart (WMT -2.22%) and its affiliates represent approximately 16% of total sales, which indicates a notable reliance on a single major retail partner. This focus on essential goods helps the company maintain a steady presence throughout the globe.

In FY 2026, revenue reached nearly $87 billion, which represents a growth rate of approximately 3.3% over the prior year. Net income for the period was nearly $16 billion. This performance highlights the ability of Procter & Gamble to maintain stable earnings and profit levels even as revenue fluctuates across its various global markets.

Nike faces intense competition in the athletic footwear and apparel market from rivals like Adidas (ADDDF -1.81%), Lululemon Athletica (LULU -0.82%), Under Armour (UAA +1.53%), V.F. (VFC +3.35%), and ASICS (ASCCF -7.79%). The company also competes with Li Ning (LNNGF +0.00%) and other international brands. Dependence on a concentrated base of contract manufacturers, primarily in Vietnam, Indonesia, and China, creates supply chain and disruption risks. Global economic volatility and currency fluctuations also significantly impact consumer spending and operational margins.

Procter & Gamble operates in a highly competitive consumer products industry with global and local rivals and private-label brands. The company is vulnerable to global supply chain disruptions, including commodity and raw material cost fluctuations and the impact of climate change. Macroeconomic and geopolitical instability, including trade controls and sanctions in countries where it operates, pose ongoing threats.

Procter & Gamble offers a more conservative valuation relative to its earnings estimates, while NIKE carries a higher price tag despite slower recent revenue growth.

Nike intrigues me as it moves away from being more of a growth stock toward a dividend stock. While this isn't often talked about, it has increased its dividend payouts for 24 consecutive years. That doesn't make it a Dividend King yet, as it would need to increase those payouts for 50 consecutive years, but it's on the path to becoming one. In addition to that consistency, the yield for that dividend is hefty, sitting at 4.1%.

The catch with Nike, however, is that sales are slumping, and some of the market may still be waiting for it to return to its high-growth ways. That mismatch between what Nike was and what it's becoming is where I see the investable opportunity. In the meantime, the stock price could continue to decline until sales stabilize, cutting into those dividend returns. That's why, between the two, I would rather own Procter & Gamble. While Nike is on the track to becoming a Dividend King, Procter already is one, having increased its payouts for 70 consecutive years.

With its essential household goods, it has also fared better than Nike thus far in 2026; Procter & Gamble shares are up 14.1%, while Nike's stock price is down 38.6%. And while Procter's dividend yield of 2.6% may be lower than Nike's yield, you aren't dealing with a stock price trying to get out of a rut.

Jack Delaney has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Nike and Walmart. The Motley Fool recommends Lululemon Athletica Inc. and Under Armour. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Hong Kong Launches Post-Quantum Preparation Toolkit for Banks

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: The Hong Kong Monetary Authority and the Hong Kong University of Science and Technology announced a post-quantum cryptography assessment toolkit on November 2. Post-quantum migration is not the same thing as making every blockchain or bank system instantly quantum-secure.

Good security planning is often unglamorous. Discovering where keys are issued, which partners rely on them and when equipment will be replaced is difficult work, but it makes future transitions possible. The banking sector has a reason to start early because data captured today can remain sensitive long after the original connection has ended.

The Hong Kong Monetary Authority and the Hong Kong University of Science and Technology announced a post-quantum cryptography assessment toolkit on November 2. The initiative is aimed at helping banks understand where current cryptographic systems might need upgrades as quantum capabilities develop. It is a preparation programme, not a declaration that quantum computers have broken banking or cryptocurrency encryption. The dated original disclosure is the starting point for the figures and product scope discussed here.

Large organisations use cryptography in certificates, customer authentication, interbank links, software updates and the storage of sensitive data. Migrating those systems can take years because algorithms, devices, vendors and legacy applications must remain interoperable. A toolkit can help identify dependencies, prioritise sensitive data and test readiness for future standards before a forced emergency transition.

The HKMA has pointed to gaps in sector readiness and ambitions to improve preparedness by 2030. The most immediate benefit may be governance: teams can assign ownership of the inventory and agree which systems require the longest lead time. That is more useful than debating a speculative date on which a general-purpose quantum computer could break a particular cryptographic scheme.

A crypto incident produces several separate timelines: containment, investigation, recovery and a return to reliable service. Those phases can overlap without finishing together. A traced wallet is not automatically frozen; a frozen balance is not automatically returned; and a reopened withdrawal route is not a completed security audit. Keeping those differences visible helps readers judge progress more accurately.

For additional perspective, it is worth reading our existing reporting on Binance’s earlier staged withdrawal restart and Bitcoin payment infrastructure and node software context. Those background reports help separate established facts from a new, still-developing announcement.

Post-quantum migration is not the same thing as making every blockchain or bank system instantly quantum-secure. Different primitives face different levels of risk, and some proposed methods affect key size, verification performance or compatibility. The toolkit’s output is an assessment input, not a substitute for independent engineering and formal testing.

Operational controls matter most when a business is under stress. Security announcements should identify the system affected, the customers who can act, the evidence supporting any recovery number and what remains uncertain. Clear scope protects users from false reassurance and from unnecessary panic. It also provides a sensible basis for follow-up accountability.

Watch whether participating institutions publish migration road maps, begin procurement changes and test post-quantum authentication with core systems. For crypto readers, it is a reminder to separate near-term operational security from longer-term cryptographic research. No credible reading of the announcement implies that Bitcoin private keys are currently being routinely cracked by quantum machines.

Philippine Migrant Worker Data Likely Leaked in 69GB Government Breach

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Breaking cybersecurity news, news analysis, commentary, and other content from around the world, with an initial focus on the Middle East & Africa, the Asia Pacific, Europe, and Latin America.

An unknown operator appears to have exfiltrated SQL Server backups from the Philippine Overseas Employment Administration in under a half hour.

An unknown threat actor managed to gain access to sensitive databases belonging to the Philippine Overseas Employment Administration, exfiltrating nearly 69GB of information on workers, contracts, and financial records, according to an analysis of a staging server used in the breach.

Over 24 minutes, the attacker copied 27 backup databases with information on overseas-employment case management, including accreditation, contracts, and cashier and LandBank records, according to threat intelligence firm Hunt.io's Oct. 28 analysis of the logs found on a Singaporean staging server used by the attacker. The cyberattackers used privileged escalation tools, namely GodPotato and SeImpersonate, and the Impacket network library for Python to move the data, but the trail stopped at the staging server, says Esteban Borges, head of research for Hunt.io.

The server had been later accessed, but by who is not clear, Borges says. While Hunt.io has not yet discovered the data as advertised for sale, the information may have been detected and removed, but that seems unlikely, he says.

Related:State Bank of India Suffers a Customer PII Data Leak

"I won't claim the backups stopped on the staging server," he says, so it's "still a breach, since the data was copied out of the agency’s control and the log shows the transfer completing."

This is not the first time that cybersecurity firms have found evidence that Philippine government data had been stolen. In August, Hunt.io discovered another threat actor's workspace hosting a number of tools and, in that case, 1.2GB of data stolen from a Philippine nuclear regulatory agency and a maritime shipbuilding contractor. A year ago, Philippine police arrested the mayor of a small town after discovering she was a Chinese national posing as a Filipina citizen and operating a cyber-scam center.

Whether the current incident involved China or cybercriminal groups is not clear. While Hunt.io did find some Chinese language fragments in the parts of the code, that code belonged to a third-party software library; thus, Hunt.io did not attribute the hack. The company did say, however, that both cybercriminals and nation-state adversaries would find value in the personally identifiable information (PII).

"That kind of migrant-worker PII, plus contract and payment data, is useful for fraud and identity theft, so it has commercial value," Borges says. "It's also a detailed map of a diaspora population, which can interest a state actor."

The latest insight into attacks on the Philippine government came after Hunt.io researchers discovered an open directory on a server hosted on the Singaporean address space of a Helsinki-based firm UpCloud Ltd., the analysis stated. The open directory contained tools and logs files for the attack, including a rebranded version of the GodPotato privilege escalation library, a PowerShell stager to unpack tools in memory, and the full Impacket Python environment.

Related:Russian Influence Operation Widely Targets Latin America

While Hunt.io considered the incident a confirmed compromise, the lack of runtime telemetry meant that Borges could not answer where the attacker moved the data.

"[W]e still can’t answer ... how they got in, whether the payload ran, and where the data went," he says.

However, the tools and the attacker's process indicated an operator with some strong ability, but who also made some simple mistakes, Borges says.

"The payload engineering is competent, [but] the operational security is poor — they left the staging server and its logs wide open," he says. "[It was] someone who can build the tool properly but was careless about keeping their own server quiet. I wouldn't call that top-tier or amateur."

The impact of the breach may boil down to the identity of the attacker and what they do with the stolen data. While Hunt.io notified the CERT Philippines (CERT-PH) and the Department of Migrant Workers (DMW), the company declined to provide any more details of the national responses to the leak.

Related:Venezuelan Cartel's Malware Honcho Nabbed for ATM Jackpotting

However, Borges did say that, so far, the information has not appeared for sale on the Dark Web.

"We haven't seen the backups surface for sale or in a leak," he says. "We never opened the backups themselves."

Governments in the region need to scan their own networks and lock down their attack surface area, he says. Workers with too many privileges allow attackers with tools such as SeImpersonate to gain more access to assets on the network. Hunt.io also found that the migrant-worker portal exposed a number of risky ports, including SMB on 445 and WinRM on 5985 and 47001.

Bangladesh: Academics Allegedly Targeted for Supporting Hasina

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Education  |  Read Source →

Click to expand Image The University of Dhaka in Bangladesh, July 1, 2021. © 2021 Sony Ramany/NurPhoto via AP Photo

(London) – Scores of academics and students at public universities in Bangladesh have been arbitrarily punished because of their alleged affiliation with former ruler Sheikh Hasina and her Awami League party, Human Rights Watch said today.

Any action to punish persons for their peaceful beliefs or without ensuring due process would violate the rights to freedom of expression and association protected under international human rights law and reproduce abusive attempts under previous administrations to control political opinion and expression at Bangladesh’s public universities.

“Bangladesh has a long history of political debate in its universities, and only autocrats should fear such discourse, not a government formed following student protests,” said Meenakshi Ganguly, deputy Asia director at Human Rights Watch. “Bangladesh needs to turn the page on the repression of the past, not repeat it whenever the ruling and opposition parties trade places.”

The sanctions—dismissals, suspension, and teaching restrictions—focus on allegations that they were complicit in violence by Hasina’s Awami League government, which was toppled by student-led protests in 2024, allegations of other “subversive” activities in support of her party, or their alleged discrimination against students wearing Islamic clothing. Hundreds of students, allegedly Awami League members and supporters, are also being expelled, denying them their education rights.

Human Rights Watch interviewed over a dozen affected teachers, examined university correspondence, and reviewed and analyzed a televised discussion and a video posted on social media. Interviews with teachers and students also confirmed expulsions, cancellation of exam certificates, or barring students from campus.

According to media reports in August 2026, at least 404 more academics at 22 universities, who were part of a pro-Awami League Telegram messaging app group, have been put under investigation. “What is happening now is that the university authorities are targeting with false allegations the group of teachers who are aligned to the Awami League and did not support the 2024 student movement,” said a professor at a public university, who is not identified for security reasons. “We are not allowed to go take classes, we cannot do research work, we cannot do anything.”

Hundreds of people were killed in a brutal police crackdown on the protests in July and August 2024, which forced Hasina to flee and paved the way for the return of the Bangladesh Nationalist Party to power following an election in February 2026. The activities of the Awami League were criminalized by an interim government in May 2025.

For years, Bangladesh’s universities have been run in a partisan manner, employing and promoting academics close to the party in government and acting against supporters of other political parties.

Starting in September 2024, the newly appointed authorities at Dhaka University, the country’s most prestigious university, ordered 44 academics to stop their work and restricted teaching assignments of another 25, without a transparent or credible disciplinary investigation. Subsequently, six of the academics were formally “temporarily suspended” following the findings of an inquiry committee, and similar investigations have now begun against another 14 teachers.

At Bangladesh Agricultural University in Mymensingh, the authorities dismissed, suspended, or removed 12 academics and demoted or restricted the activities of another 34 after setting up an “investigation commission,” though this did not appear to comply with the university’s legally established disciplinary process. And in Sher-e-Bangla Agricultural University in Dhaka, 12 academics have been temporarily suspended and another 10 removed from academic activities following an inquiry process that teachers said they were not allowed to attend, an apparent breach of the law covering academic discipline. Another 85 teachers were denied promotions.

In all, over 20 public universities have been affected, Human Rights Watch found.

In March 2025, Dhaka University temporarily suspended 126 students, and withheld their exam certificates, after a fact-finding committee said it had found photographic, video, or social media evidence that they had attacked peaceful protesters. Cases filed with the police allege that another 277 Awami League students were involved. The university authorities have taken no formal action against them, but students and teachers confirmed that they stay away from campus for fear of arrest. Many of those named deny any role in the violence, yet the university has not provided a forum for them to answer the allegations.

Among the academics affected is Professor Mashiur Rahman, from the Dhaka University sociology department, who since 2017 was deputed as a vice-chancellor at another university. He was told to stop all academic and administrative work on September 25, 2024. The allegations against him include claims that during the protests he “directly opposed the movement, calling it illegal, baseless and irrational,” during a July 2024 television discussion, although a video of the broadcast, reviewed by Human Rights Watch, shows no such comment.

Another allegation is that at the prime minister’s house on August 3, 2024, he “incited genocide.” However, this was a meeting set up by the University Grants Commission that all vice-chancellors had to attend, and he has told Human Rights Watch that no evidence has been provided to him showing that he made such a statement, which he denies. On August 31, 2026, the university authorities recommended permanently suspending him, which he is appealing.

The allegations against the sanctioned teachers at the Bangladesh Agriculture University center on their participation in a pro-Awami League march on August 4, 2024, the day before Hasina fled the country. A report by a Commission of Inquiry set up by the university refers to the march as a “terrorist” procession.

A video posted on Facebook by the Bangladesh Agricultural University Journalists’ Association on August 4, 2024, and analyzed by Human Rights Watch, shows a scene from the march. In the video, a group can be seen walking on the campus chanting slogans. No direct confrontations are visible. Professor Purba Islam from the Department of Pharmacology was suspended on August 4, 2025, for her “sloganizing” at the procession, alleging “support for the oppression, persecution and genocide committed by the fascist and autocrat Sheikh Hasina.” No evidence of her shouting slogans is visible in the video.

Professor Yahia Khandoker, from the university’s Department of Animal Breeding and Genetics, was also suspended on August 4, 2025, accused of maintaining “on the university campus, a clear position in favor of Sheikh Hasina’s mass killing.” Throughout the whole protest period, he said, he was with his family in Dhaka, not at the university. The High Court has stayed his suspension, though the Attorney General’s Office is appealing.

Three academics, two of them in prison, were convicted by the country’s International Crimes Tribunal on crimes against humanity charges, with another awaiting trial.

“Any acts of revenge against people who peacefully supported the previous government would show that freedoms of speech and assembly are not protected,” Ganguly said. “Universities should be places where people are free to express their views, not places where support is enforced for the government of the day.”
